| Individual→Circle | Expression, action, refusal, exit, or bridging | Resource flows, imitation by members, institutional write-back, network propagation, and a mechanism of representation | Changes in rules, boundaries, allocation, relationships, or collective states | The action is not received, or the change in the circle precedes the individual action |
| Circle↔Individual Feedback | The two chains above form a continuing closed loop | Return channel, delay, thresholds, and traces | Changes in transition probabilities in subsequent rounds | Only a signal arrives, without changing any subsequent state |

“Affect one another” is only the beginning of an explanation. The analyst must still look for direction, channels, delays, thresholds, losses, and counterfactuals.

## 9.6 Evidence Levels and Upgrading Variable Status

Actor variables are upgraded in a conservative order. A model can propose candidates from residuals. It must not promote a candidate directly to a fact. A single self-report, a single account from another person, a single behavioral observation, and a single measurement scale result each carry different biases. Agreement across sources does not automatically rule out a common environment, a shared narrative, or the same measurement bias.

| Current Status | Permitted Use | Requirements for Upgrading | What Must Be Retained |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Unknown | Raise questions and flag impacts | Lawful and appropriate new observations | The kind of unknown and the reason for leaving the information uncollected |
| Candidate | Use in scenario projection and design a minimal test | Predeclared indicators, time window, and counterexample check | Competing candidates, privacy, and risks |
| Supported hypothesis | Use for explanation or foresight in limited contexts | Out-of-sample review, tests across contexts, or independent sources | Limits of applicability, counterevidence, and error |
| Observation record | Describe behavior or a state at a specific time | Integrity of sources and an observation contract | Do not promote behavior to personality |
| Disputed | Present conflicting materials side by side and lower the strength of the output | Independent review or new discriminating evidence | The positions and interests of all parties |
| Retired | Preserve the history and exclude it from the current projection | A new version may append only the reasons for retirement | Impacts caused by the earlier judgment and remedies |

If a variable is proposed only after results are known, it can help explain those results and generate a test for the next round. It cannot retroactively support the original prediction. If the definition, time band, or indicators of the same variable change, a new version should be created. Earlier failures must not be overwritten.

## 9.7 Material and Experience–Meaning Conditions in Actor States

An actor’s material conditions include physical load, observable aspects of health and sleep, time, space, money, tools, transportation, care responsibilities, standing under the law, and the actual ability to exit. Experience–meaning conditions include feelings, expectations, memory, identity, trust, shame, hope, fear, commitments, perceived legitimacy, and conflicts of value.

Record the two groups separately according to their relevance to the current question. If the task only needs an adequate technical or quantitative indicator, collection of irrelevant private experiences must not be compelled. If the conclusion concerns life opportunities, meaning, or autonomy, material totals cannot substitute for the corresponding experiences. Material deprivation can increase pressure without automatically determining how the actor interprets meaning. Positive beliefs can increase persistence but cannot remove constraints imposed by resources, the body, or institutions. An inference across channels must specify which material change affects behavior through which experiential mechanism, or which change in meaning alters material conditions through which action.

Status also has an institutional dimension that cannot be reduced to personal experience. Self-identification, classification by others, administrative registration, legal eligibility, and actual treatment must be recorded separately. A designation can alter access to hiring, services, the submission of evidence, or appeals, even if the person does not accept it. Institutional standing and the experience of dignity can affect one another without either establishing the other. The categories and comparison groups used by researchers must be open to challenge by those concerned. The versions of both must also be open to review.

A person can still act under constraints. A person signing under a credible threat and a device moving their body to produce the same handwriting involve different processes. The first can still be the person’s own action, even though there is no safe way to refuse and valid consent is absent. Attribution of the action, knowledge, control, feasible alternatives, blame, and remedy each require their own grounds. A causal explanation does not automatically exempt anyone from responsibility or establish that blame is warranted.

## 9.8 Privacy, Absence, and Refusal to Disclose

The closer the analysis gets to an individual, the higher the cost of misuse. Actor records must distinguish information that is public, limited to a particular context, sensitive, or highly sensitive, as well as information a person declines to disclose. Permitted uses must be consistent with the purpose of collection, authorization, and protections for those affected. Using material for communication does not by itself qualify it for use in hiring, insurance, law enforcement, medical care, punishment, or public evaluation.

Refusal to disclose is not evidence of hostility, deception, ignorance, or risk. Silence can stem from safety concerns, trauma, privacy, language, trust, differences in power, or reasonable noncooperation. The analyst may record only that “there is no usable information” and how this limits projection. Personality inferences must not be used to fill the gap.

## 9.9 Actor State Snapshots

An auditable snapshot must contain at least the following.

| Section | What to Record | Output Ceiling |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Observation boundaries | Actor, time window, observation position, and data cutoff | Limit to the current sample |
| Circles and roles | Memberships, roles, conflicts, and ability to enter and exit | Do not assume identification with the circle or common goals |
| Slow, intermediate-timescale, and fast variables | Status, source, time, and counterevidence for each variable | Do not judge the essence of personality |
| Conditions in both channels | Material conditions and experience–meaning conditions | Do not substitute either for the other |
| Competing explanations | Structural factors, roles, strategies, measurement, and chance | Retain explanations that cannot be distinguished |
| Privacy and uses | Privacy level, authorization, and prohibited uses | Stay within the limits on use |
| Update conditions | Which variables a new event reaches and when to review them | Do not update every state at once |

The snapshot retains its observation cutoff and the time at which information was obtained. When a new event occurs, first determine which channels and time bands it reaches and then update the corresponding variables. Calculate autonomous changes triggered by the passage of time itself separately under the registered rules for dynamics. When neither a new observation nor a reliable rule for advancing the state is available, the last observed value and its time can be retained or the state can be marked unknown. The old value must not be treated as the actual current state. Rerunning the model is likewise no evidence that all variables changed at once.

## 9.10 Example of Actor Analysis in an Event

Suppose a project lead suddenly vetoes the team’s proposal in a public meeting. The observation record can establish only the veto, the context of the meeting, what was said, and the visible consequences. Candidate fast variables might include time pressure, physical fatigue, perceived threat, and immediate anger. Candidate intermediate-timescale variables might include project goals, responsibilities of the role, a recent decline in trust, and reputational risk. A candidate slow variable might be long-term tolerance of uncertainty, which would require evidence across contexts.

The circles must also be examined. Has management just changed the metrics? Is the team short on resources? Has public opinion on a platform raised the cost of failing publicly? Do care responsibilities at home reduce the time available? Does organizational culture reward forceful decisions? The lead’s veto may change trust and subsequent expression in the team. The team’s silence may reinforce the lead’s belief that “only coercion works,” creating feedback. Writing only “controlling personality” would obscure multiple channels open to intervention. Writing only “determined by structure” would erase the actor’s choices, responsibilities, and capability to bridge circles.

A reasonable output should separate confirmed facts about behavior from the support available for hypotheses about states. It should also specify circle conditions and channels in both directions, the next observation that would best distinguish the alternatives, and what remains unknown when evidence is insufficient. The output can enter path projection in Part 11. It cannot itself produce a diagnosis of the person, punishment, or authority to compel.

## 9.11 Where This Part Stops

The actor state layer can describe which observable states and candidate variables are currently available. It can identify changes that roles and circles may activate and the channels through which individuals and circles constitute one another. It can also describe which time bands a new event may reach first and which information remains unknown or protected by privacy.

This framework does not adjudicate the essence of a person. No professional assessment amounts to an ontological definition of a person. The clinical significance and scope of application of personality descriptions should be assessed by qualified professionals on the basis of relevant evidence, valid authorization, and participation by the people concerned. This framework cannot supply that assessment on its own. Future paths enter conditional foresight. Value and action enter the layer of normative choice and authorization. Prediction, professional status, and labels do not automatically confer authority to act.

[^tn-actor-tuple-10]: Translator's note: The full tuple in English is `A* = <actor ID, observation window, circle memberships, roles, slow variables, intermediate-timescale variables, fast variables, material conditions, experience–meaning conditions, unknowns, evidential status, privacy and permitted uses>`.

[^tn-circle-10]: Translator's note: Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, or bridged.

<a id="part_10"></a>

# Part 10 Multi-Circle Objects and Joint States

Analyzing a single object or circle[^tn-circle-11] often compresses other conditions into a black box called “environment.” Multi-circle analysis brings into one joint state the structural units that can change the path of an event and that can be distinguished by boundaries, relations, channels, and time windows. The aim does not require adding background without limit. Families, teams, institutions, platforms, markets, cultural communities, bodily systems, and geographic spaces can all matter in the same event. Naming them does not automatically supply grounds for object facts about them or support for a grouping gain.

## 10.1 The Candidate Object Contract[^tn-contract-11] for a Circle

A circle can be represented as follows.

`C* = <圈层 ID，候选边界，成员资格，角色规则，进入与退出，内部状态，外部接口，物质通道，体验—意义通道，约束，时间窗，尺度剖面，同一性判据，证据状态，反例>`.[^tn-circle-tuple-11]

“Several similar people” does not suffice to define a circle. A circle need not have shared consciousness or a unified goal. Members may not know that the analyst has grouped them into the same circle. They may disagree about goals, and a candidate structure may form solely through shared exposure to a platform, rule, space, or risk. Documents, memberships, sustained interactions, or shared exposure can support the corresponding facts about the object. A further claim that this grouping improves explanation or foresight requires testing for a gain within the stated scope against a complexity-matched alternative grouping or no-grouping baseline. Support under G1 concerns eligibility for this latter task-specific claim. It does not decide whether the organization, relationships, or people affected exist.

| Admission Question | Evidence Required | If the Check Does Not Pass |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Can the boundary be distinguished? | Membership rules, interfaces, shared exposure, or sustained interaction | Retain it as the analyst’s grouping and do not call it an objective circle |
| Is there differentiated structure within it? | Roles, relations, channels, or constraints | Reclassify it as a population category or an environmental variable |
| If a grouping gain is claimed, does the grouping improve explanation or foresight? | Out-of-sample comparison with a simple baseline | Do not claim that a gain has been established. Retain facts about the object that have independent grounds. Depending on the task, fall back to a single focus, continuous variables, or a candidate grouping |
| Does identity hold within the time window? | K, entry and exit, and conditions for conversion and dissolution | Record separate segments or register object conversion |
| Have counterexamples been retained? | Boundary perturbation or review across time or locations | Report the sensitivity and narrow the related conclusions; a grouping gain already claimed is handled under §4.3 |

## 10.2 The Joint Object Contract

The multi-circle joint object is written as follows.

`MC* = <行动者集合 A，圈层集合 C，圈层关系 Rcc，成员与角色映射 Rac，物质状态 M，体验—意义状态 Ψ，通道与约束 Q，事件流 E，多时钟 T，尺度剖面 SP，证据状态 W，同一性判据 K>`.[^tn-joint-tuple-11]

The value of a joint object lies in preserving local differences and cross-boundary channels. It does not put all related objects into one large circle or assert that a higher-level subject exists. Circles with no identifiable relation may only be observed side by side. If they share only an environment, a direct effect should not be invented. If the relation appears only during an event, register it as temporary and state the conditions under which it ends.

| Joint State Field | Responsibility | Common Error |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A Actor set | Preserve actor states, roles, and privacy boundaries | Substituting a circle average for the individual |
| C Circle set | Maintain boundaries, states, and identity separately | Merging all background conditions into one overarching circle |
| Rcc Circle relations | Register direction, type, delay, threshold, and evidence | Substituting “interrelated” for a mechanism |
| Rac Membership and role mapping | Allow multiple affiliations, role conflicts, and differences in ability to exit | Assuming each person belongs to only one circle |
| M and Ψ | Represent material and experience–meaning conditions side by side | Material determinism or reduction to narrative alone |
| Q Channels and constraints | Specify the extent and capacity of propagation | Assuming impacts are lossless, instantaneous, and reach every position |
| E and T | Event stream and multiple asynchronous clocks | Updating every state synchronously after each event |
| W and K | Evidence, unknowns, residuals, and object identity | Mistaking a complete model for a complete account of reality |

## 10.3 Six Kinds of Circle Relation

The six starting points for description distinguish different questions. They are nonexclusive, address different dimensions, and do not exhaust real-world relations. Parallelism, nesting, and overlap describe positions along a declared dimension. Bridging and competition describe connections or constraints. Temporariness describes duration and conditions for ending. These descriptions can be combined. For each actual circle relation, separately record the source circle, target circle, direction, shared members or interfaces, channel, extent, time window, and relevant evidence. Do not force a value for an inapplicable delay or threshold.

### 10.3.1 Parallelism

Under the current contract, parallel circles have no relation of containment in either membership or the scope of their operating contracts. The circles may still be related. Two parallel teams may share a budget, a platform, and higher-level institutional arrangements. They may also be triggered simultaneously by the same event. If only shared exposure can be confirmed, register a shared environment. Register cross-circle propagation only when a channel for information, resources, personnel, or institutional write-back has been found.

### 10.3.2 Nesting

Nesting means that one object’s extent is actually contained within another along an explicitly specified relational dimension. The basis of containment must be stated. Membership, roles, resource accounting, institutional jurisdiction, and space are distinct dimensions. If the claim concerns containment of only a part, first identify the contained subobject and its boundary. If two sets intersect but neither contains the other, register overlap on that dimension. Describing the intersection as “partial containment” does not also justify classifying it as nesting. A subcircle’s state cannot directly represent the higher-level circle, and the outer circle’s rules need not take effect in the same way at every position inside it.

### 10.3.3 Overlap

Overlap means that circles share some members, roles, resources, or interfaces without fully containing one another. Positions of overlap may take on bridging, translation, and coordination. They may also face role conflict, conflicting loyalties, duplicated work, and risks of information leakage. The proportion of overlap cannot substitute for a record of what is actually shared.

### 10.3.4 Bridging

Bridging means that an actor, institution, interface, text, platform, or event connects previously separate circles. A bridging party need not have authority to represent others or be loyal to either side. Transmission capability, translation losses, selective filtering, responsibility, substitutability, overload, and exit conditions must be registered separately.

### 10.3.5 Competition

Competition means that circles create identifiable exclusionary constraints on scarce resources, attention, legitimacy, member commitments, interpretive authority, or room for action. Different goals do not automatically amount to competition. Register competition only when one circle’s use of what is available, its rules, or its actions changes the set available to another circle. Cooperation and competition can coexist through different channels.

### 10.3.6 Temporariness

Temporary circles form around events, tasks, crises, shared exposure, or short-term goals. They may dissolve rapidly or become more durable through traces, stabilization of roles, and institutionalization. Temporariness may follow from a task deadline set in advance or be a provisional judgment based on the short window observed so far. The two should be distinguished. A temporary relation can be important, carry responsibilities, and already be real. A temporary alliance can still overlap in membership, compete for resources, and be bridged through particular interfaces.

| Relation | Minimum Criterion | What to Check in Particular | Failure or Change |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Parallelism | No containment relation at present | Shared environment and indirect coupling | Containment, overlap, or bridging emerges |
| Nesting | Explicit containment in membership/contract/jurisdiction/space | Downward constraints and loss from upward aggregation | K changes, exit occurs, or jurisdiction is reconfigured |
| Overlap | Some members, roles, resources, or interfaces are shared | Role conflict and double counting | Shared items disappear or the relation becomes containment |
| Bridging | Identifiable interface for cross-circle transmission | Translation, filtering, overload, and authority to represent | The channel closes, is replaced, or becomes institutionalized |
| Competition | An exclusionary constraint on the available set | Scarce items, time window, and channels for cooperation | Resources expand, rules are coordinated, or a party exits |
| Temporariness | A clearly finite duration or support so far limited to a short window. Distinguish the two | Ending, traces, and who takes up responsibility. Temporariness can be combined with other dimensions of relation | The temporary relation ends, is extended, or becomes institutionalized. Its positions and channel relations are recorded separately |

“Relation change” records a shift in a relation’s type or defining conditions between the states before and after an event. Referring back to the relation by the same name does not by itself establish that it has changed. Relations can persist, strengthen, weaken, change direction, change type, or dissolve. Every judgment must be tied to snapshots before and after, the dimension that changed, and object identity.

## 10.4 Relations Do Not Form a Single Tree

Actual multi-circle structures typically form a directed multigraph of relations with local containment. The same family can be nested within a community and within a legal system. A person can belong at the same time to overlapping family, work, platform, and interest circles. Platforms may bridge parallel groups. Different organizations can compete for funding, cooperate on safety, and overlap in personnel.

The framework does not require each node to have only one parent node. Nor does “higher level” automatically mean greater importance, reality, or authority. Each edge expresses just one declared relation. If a pair of circles has multiple edges, record the channel and time window for each edge separately. If edges at different scales are incomparable, keep them side by side without forcing an order.

The graph representation must pass at least four checks. Has the analyst’s classification been mistaken for an object? Has a shared environment been mistaken for a direct relation? Has membership overlap been mistaken for organizational containment? Has contact with information been mistaken for effective feedback? Register feedback only when a signal returns and changes a subsequent state, transition probability, or constraint.

## 10.5 Multiple Memberships and Role Conflicts

Membership cannot be reduced to a binary, permanent status. Records should include the basis for membership, start and end times, roles, strength of commitment, actual participation, ability to exit, and disputes. Formal members may lack the capability to act. Informal participants may bear key costs. People drawn in involuntarily may be unable to leave. Representatives may lack authorization from the people they represent.

| Membership Dimension | Example States | Implication for Projection |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Basis for entry | Voluntary, employment, kinship, law, platform rules, shared exposure, or coercion | Determines the limits of responsibility and exit |
| Extent of participation | Core, intermittent, peripheral, nominal, or passively bearing the consequences | Determines visible information and channels of impact |
| Role | Decision-making, execution, care, bridging, oversight, affected party, or dissent | Determines rights, obligations, and conflicts |
| Commitment | High, medium, low, disputed, or unknown | Affects paths without amounting to personality |
| Ability to exit | Free, costly, subject to approval, or impossible in practice | Triggers the protection floor and the action ceiling |

An actor’s multiple memberships do not automatically establish actual bridging. Potential bridging capability, actual transmission across boundaries, and outcomes caused by transmission are recorded separately. An identifiable interface supports a description of capability. Records of actual transmission support a description of the process. A causal effect on state requires a corresponding comparison. If recipients do not change their choices, this does not negate the limited transmission of information that already occurred. Authority to represent, transmission capability, and responsible parties must be kept distinct.

## 10.6 Material Conditions and Experience–Meaning Conditions

The joint state maintains both M and Ψ. M includes the body, time, space, money, energy, equipment, infrastructure, property rights, supply, care, and actual capability to act. Ψ includes feelings, expectations, memory, identity, trust, shame, hope, fear, commitments, conflicts of value, and perceived legitimacy.

The two channels can affect one another. For example, a platform’s rules for traffic change visibility and income, affecting material and informational conditions. Members interpret the decline as exclusion or failure, a change in experience and meaning. This interpretation may affect exit, protest, or cooperation and thereby feed back into platform metrics and resource allocation. Each step must specify a carrier, direction, delay, and counterexamples.

| Bridge Between Channels | Starting Point | Mediating Factors | Outcome | Counterfactual Question |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| M→Ψ | A change in income, space, the body, or time | Sense of safety, sense of control, or interpretation of identity | Changes in trust, fear, or commitment | Do interpretations differ under the same material change? |
| Ψ→M | Legitimacy, hope, shame, or conflicts of value | Cooperation, refusal, migration, care, or protest | Changes in resource flows, labor, property rights, or use of facilities | Would the action occur without the change in meaning? |
| M↔Ψ Closed loop | Effects in both directions occur in succession | Feedback and traces | Path dependence or phase transition | Does the return signal actually change a subsequent state? |

Analyzing material and experience–meaning conditions side by side requires neither equal weights nor full collection on both sides for every narrow task. If the objective concerns members’ control, well-being, or relationships, relevant differences on both sides should be retained. For a question such as the total amount of equipment, for which an adequate summary is already available, the analysis may stop within that scope.

### 10.6.1 Resource Conversion and Multiple Positions

Resources create usable opportunities only under specific conditions for conversion. Income pays current expenses. Wealth provides a buffer or collateral. Qualifications and skills need recognition from the relevant institutions. Relationships must be possible to mobilize, and the other party must actually have resources and be willing to help. Transportation, time, care, standing under the law, information, and the cost of refusal may cause equal amounts of resources to yield different practical options. These quantities cannot be added into a single capital or power score without specifying units, conversion rules, double counting, weights, and purpose. A well-grounded monetary valuation or an index for a specific task can answer the question within its limits. A total cannot fill in each person’s control, qualifications, or well-being. A nominal allocation cannot substitute for resources actually at the recipient’s disposal.

The combined effects of multiple positions need not take the form of simple overlap among several circles. Rules of admission, accumulation of seniority, evaluation, and redress within the same institutional arrangement may change a group’s opportunities in succession. For example, later entry into a position where seniority accrues can continue to produce differences when opportunities are subsequently allocated uniformly by seniority. The sequence of rules, the denominators for entry at each stage, comparison groups, and positions that are absent should be traced. The number of categories, an interaction term, or a group average does not by itself establish the cause of harm to an individual.

Different statuses must not be put in a fixed order of disadvantage in advance. The same cue may receive different responses in different jobs, locations, relationships, or periods. Retain differences within categories, those who did not enter, and those who succeeded. Adding sensitive classifications is warranted only if it can answer a clear question, the material is sufficient to support it, and the privacy cost is tolerable. Do not compel disclosure to fill a cross-tabulation or treat the positions as nonexistent because the material cannot be obtained.

### 10.6.2 Assets, Liabilities, Contracts, and Transmission Through Credit

In financial relations, the material state must at least distinguish stocks at a point in time from flows over a period, asset valuations from funds that can be converted into cash, principal payable from interest, and net worth from the ability to refinance. Assets in excess of liabilities do not guarantee that cash will be available when payments are due. A new loan does not by itself repair a shortfall in asset value. The market price of collateral, the contractual borrowing limit, the actual outstanding loan balance, and recovery on default are distinct quantities. Each must be tied to the currency, maturity, contract, repayment priority, and observation window.

One testable path of amplification starts with changes in assets or cash flow that alter net worth and the amount that can be pledged as collateral. Through loan quotes, credit limits, loan extensions, or margin calls, these changes affect net financing. Changes in financing then affect consumption, investment, hiring, and subsequent income and feed back into demand for assets. A candidate for self-reinforcement may be proposed only when the relevant constraints are binding and cash, equity, alternative loans, or arrangements for bearing risk have not cut the chain. Earnings expectations, interest rates, or policy may also drive prices and spending jointly. Changes in the same direction are insufficient to identify a financial accelerator mechanism.

In a financial network, common exposure, defaults on direct claims, calls on contractual guarantees, price spillovers from fire sales, and withdrawals of funds prompted by information must be recorded separately. Risk transfer changes who holds the risk and what they can bear. Whether it reduces overall risk still depends on the receiving party’s capital, contingent obligations, and who ultimately bears losses. The face values of mutual claims and liabilities across multiple layers must not be added up as net wealth for the system. Resumption of payments, restoration of capital, recovery of borrowers’ cash flow, and recovery of living conditions are different outcomes.

## 10.7 Five Kinds of Clock and Asynchronous Updating

A joint state distinguishes at least immediate, interactive, organizational, institutional, and long-term clocks. Immediate reactions may be measured in minutes, trust in interaction in rounds, organizational budgets in quarters, institutional write-back in deliberation cycles, and long-term learning or generational change in years. The timing of the same event’s effects varies by variable.

| Clock | Typical Objects | Update Trigger | Common Error |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Immediate | Emotion, attention, bodily arousal, local prices | Arrival of a signal or a direct shock | Recording short-term fluctuations as long-term change |
| Interactive | Trust, strategies, expectations in relationships, local norms | Several rounds of action and feedback | Ignoring the order of rounds and memory |
| Organizational | Roles, budgets, processes, metrics | Cycles of decision, execution, and review | Assuming a decision amounts to implementation |
| Institutional | Rules, rights, responsibilities, appeals, records | Actual change in rules or procedures, actual write-back, and execution. Record authorization and legal validity separately | Treating a verbal commitment as institutional change |
| Long-term | Capabilities, identity, infrastructure, culture, and relations across generations | Accumulation, learning, maintenance, and replacement | Extrapolating to the long term from short-term data |

External events directly update only variables reached through their channels and for which the conditions hold. Endogenous processes, expiration, accumulation, and recovery proceed on their own clocks and under the registered rules for dynamics. A variable untouched by the current event is not necessarily unchanged in reality. Without reliable grounds for an update, retain the last observed value, its time, and uncertainty, or mark it unknown. Do not refresh all variables at once just to make the joint snapshot “complete.”

Asynchrony can also change the direction of effects. Asset prices can change immediately, while loan contracts adjust only upon revaluation or renewal. The timing of payments differs between fixed-rate and floating-rate debt. Public guarantees may provide cash first and distribute losses later. A joint projection should check when each constraint actually binds, when alternative financing is possible, and whether the recovery window precedes irreversible harm. Extending a short-term backstop must not be treated as restoration of the long-term ability to repay.

## 10.8 Joint State Snapshots

A snapshot is a frozen starting point for projection. It does not permanently reproduce reality. It must include a timestamp, evidence cutoff, actor and circle references, relation and membership mappings, M/Ψ states, channels, constraints, the five kinds of clock, scale profile, unknowns, residuals, and identity criteria.

Each value in a snapshot should be marked as observed, supported hypothesis, candidate, disputed, unknown, or not applicable. When sources conflict, first retain their respective values, how they were produced, and their versions. A combined estimate and its uncertainty may be reported separately only if there are grounds for both the measured object and the model used to combine results. An arithmetic mean cannot resolve the original conflict by itself. If a circle is internally differentiated, do not represent all positions with a single mean. If necessary, establish subcircles or use a distributional representation, but go through object admission again.

## 10.9 Boundary Perturbation and Object Conversion

Multi-circle models can easily mistake chosen boundaries for natural boundaries. At least three kinds of perturbation must be tested. Change the membership threshold and check whether the conclusion is stable. Move the time window and check whether the relation still holds. Replace or remove a circle and check whether the joint model’s gain disappears. If small changes in the boundary reverse the conclusion, narrow the conclusions that depend on that boundary and report the sensitivity; a grouping gain already claimed is recorded under §4.3 as not supported or unresolved.

If a circle’s K no longer holds after an event, object conversion or dissolution should be registered. For example, a temporary rescue group may become a formal institution, a project team may split into competing alliances, a platform community may migrate to a new platform, or a household’s care arrangements may be reorganized after a member exits. A new object cannot overwrite the old object’s failures, responsibilities, or historical traces.

## 10.10 The Complexity Check for Joint States

Adding circles increases the ability to fit the data and also increases error, data requirements, and degrees of freedom in explanation. Each added circle or relation must be assessed on which residual in the original model it explains, whether it has an independently observable channel, and whether it improves out-of-sample explanation or foresight. Its measurement costs and privacy risks must also be specified, and the conclusion checked with that circle or relation removed.

Multi-circle models must be compared with a simple single-focus or single-circle baseline. If a gain in prediction or explanation is claimed but the complex model only tells a fuller story about the materials already observed, without stable comparative support, downgrade the claim of gain and prefer an adequate simpler model. Keeping an object to describe established responsibilities, differences among members, or lawful permissions need not first establish a predictive gain. The independent question it answers should still be stated, recording costs should be controlled, and more data should not be treated as better explanation. Complexity does not amount to maturity. Using one fewer circle is usually better if no important distinction is lost.

## 10.11 An Example of a Joint Object

An analysis of an employee’s public departure from a job can identify the actor, project team, company management, family care circle, professional community, and public opinion on a platform at the same time. The team is nested within the company. The employee belongs to the overlapping team, family, and professional circles. The platform bridges the employee and the public outside the company. The team and management may compete over resource allocation but cooperate in maintaining reputation. A group that forms around the departure to express support may be a temporary circle.

Material conditions include pay, working hours, care, contracts, job positions, and platform traffic. Experience–meaning conditions include dignity, a sense of betrayal, professional identity, trust in coworkers, and perceived legitimacy among the public. The departure immediately changes attention and emotion, then affects trust and handover in the team. Organizational processes may change weeks later, and institutional rules may be written back later still. Any single-track explanation that the person “left because of their personality” or that the departure was “entirely determined by company structure” must be compared with the joint state.

The snapshot makes different paths visible. If the company responds only through public relations without changing working hours or appeal channels, emotions on the platform may subside while internal distrust persists. If resources are adjusted but repair of meaning fails, people may stay while their commitments continue to weaken. If the pressure of family care is lifted, the actor’s available options change. If the professional community provides resources through a bridge, the cost of exit decreases and others may follow the example. The next Part uses event-driven projection to examine which path the evidence supports.

## 10.12 Where This Part Stops

The layer for multi-circle joint states can identify which circles need to be represented together and whether their relations are parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, competitive, or temporary. It can also describe how memberships and roles are distributed across circles, how material and experience–meaning conditions are represented side by side, and which clocks are out of sync. It can assess whether the complex model offers a gain relative to a simple baseline.

A structural graph alone cannot establish what must happen in the future. Nor can it decide which circle most warrants protection, who should bear the costs, or who has authority to act. Event propagation, branching, and cascades belong to Part 11. Conditional foresight and bounded choice belong to Part 12. Normative judgments, authorization, and protection remain the responsibility of later layers.

If an event involves only one actor and one stable circle, and neighboring circles, membership overlap, bridging, and cross-boundary spillovers provide no additional information, do not use the full joint model. Multi-circle modeling makes it possible to preserve distinctions where interactions actually occur. An analysis need not bear that complexity every time. When fewer objects, fewer variables, and clearer evidence suffice to answer the question, the framework should scale back.

[^tn-circle-tuple-11]: Translator's note: The full tuple in English is `C* = <circle ID, candidate boundary, membership eligibility, role rules, entry and exit, internal state, external interfaces, material channels, experience–meaning channels, constraints, time window, scale profile, identity criterion, evidential status, counterexamples>`.

[^tn-joint-tuple-11]: Translator's note: The full tuple in English is `MC* = <actor set A, circle set C, circle relations Rcc, membership and role mapping Rac, material state M, experience–meaning state Ψ, channels and constraints Q, event stream E, multiple clocks T, scale profile SP, evidential status W, identity criterion K>`.

[^tn-circle-11]: Translator's note: Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, or bridged.

[^tn-contract-11]: Translator's note: A contract here is an analytical agreement registered and frozen before results, akin to pre-registration.

<a id="part_11"></a>

# Part 11 Event-Driven Dynamic Projection

Dynamic projection starts from a joint state with evidential statuses recorded. It analyzes state differences, transmission, feedback, and branching after observed, reported, planned, hypothetical, or simulated events. Actions that actually occur can change reality with or without authorization. They must all be recorded according to the evidence. Whether an action occurred, how it is evaluated normatively, any legal or institutional authority, and permission to carry it out on this occasion must each be judged separately. Projection must specify which changes can be established directly, which are only conditional consequences, and which signals would support or weaken the corresponding paths.

## 11.1 The Difference Between Projection and Narrative Continuation

Narrative continuation can choose what comes next by coherence alone. Structural projection must preserve conditions, channels, competing paths, and the meaning of failure. If key variables are missing, the projection should stop at an unknown or generate variable candidates. It must not fill the gaps with the smoothest story. If several continuations are consistent with existing evidence, keep them as separate branches. If paths are incomparable, do not force an ordering.

The minimum inputs to projection are a frozen snapshot, event records, mechanism hypotheses, parameter ranges, a clock policy, a propagation policy, unknowns, and stopping conditions. The minimum outputs are a path graph, state differences at each step, support status, early signals, contrary signals, residuals, variable candidates, and write-back requirements.

| Input | What Must Be Frozen | Consequence If Left Unfrozen |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Joint snapshot | Actors, circles[^tn-circle-12], relations, M/Ψ, clocks, and evidence cutoff | The starting point is rewritten after the results |
| Event | Type, time, source, objects reached, and channels | Rumors, plans, and facts are conflated |
| Mechanism | Direction, carrier, delay, threshold, and failure conditions | Each outcome can be explained arbitrarily |
| Parameter | Value, interval, level, or explicit unknown | False precision masks uncertainty |
| Stopping | Conditions involving information, risk, exceeding bounds, and resources | The story continues indefinitely |

## 11.2 The Event Contract[^tn-contract-12]

There are five kinds of event: observed, reported, planned, hypothetical, and simulated. The type must be included in the record. A reported event can affect people who believe it but must not automatically be treated as having occurred. A plan may change expectations without amounting to execution. A hypothetical event serves conditional analysis. A simulated event belongs only to a model run and must not be written back as a fact about reality.

An event record must include at least the event ID, time or window of occurrence, time of observation, source, affected actors, affected circles, channels, direct changes, evidential status, disputes, competing explanations, reversibility, duration, and subsequent observations.

| Event Type | Permitted Uses in Projection | Factual Status | Required Limits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Observed | Explanation, replay, and forward projection | Holds within the observation contract | Sources, omissions, and measurement error |
| Reported | Propagation of belief and conditional projection | That a report was made does not establish that its contents are true | The reporting party’s position, verification, and disputes |
| Planned | Expectations, preparations, and strategic interaction | Existence of the plan does not establish execution | Authorization, resources, and conditions for cancellation |
| Hypothetical | Counterfactuals and scenario comparison | No factual status in reality | Conditions, purpose, and feasibility |
| Simulated | Unfolding paths within the model | Model state only | Model version, parameters, and prohibition on extrapolation |

## 11.3 The Joint State Update Formula

The joint state can be written as follows.

`Ω(t) = <A(t), C(t), R(t), M(t), Ψ(t), Q(t), E≤t, SP(t), W(t), K(t)>`.

An update can be written as follows.

`Ω(t+Δ) = F[Ω(t), e(t), u(t), ξ(t) | θ, h]`.

Here `e(t)` is the event, `u(t)` is an externally authorized action that actually occurred, `ξ(t)` is an exogenous disturbance or unmodeled residual, `θ` denotes the frozen hypotheses about mechanisms and parameters, and `h` is the historical term. The formula specifies only what must be recorded. It does not assert that there is a unique true F, and the function symbol must not be used in place of specific mechanisms.

Observed violations, actions beyond authorization, refusals to carry out orders, and unauthorized acts in another’s stead are recorded as `observed/reported` events under `e(t)` with their evidence. The actor, consequences, and status of their authority must be recorded separately. The lack of authority must not be grounds for deleting an event from the factual record. Violations that have not occurred enter only `hypothetical/simulated` branches. The definition of `u(t)` still requires authorization and actual occurrence. Feasibility in a scenario does not amount to permission in reality. 〔Studies 15.08, 21.03, 21.08, 28.06, 32.08〕

Each update must distinguish directly observed state differences, inferences supported by the mechanism contract, scenario values set to explore a path, and residuals still unexplained. All four retain their provenance in subsequent paths. Appearing together in a snapshot does not give them the same evidential status.

### 11.3.1 States, Observations, and Identified Sets

A joint snapshot is a structured representation of the material currently available. It does not directly read every state of the world. Let `z(t)` denote the actual state yet to be inferred for the current task, and `o(t)` the record produced by observation rule H, sampling positions, and conditions of data collection. It can be written as `o(t)=H[z(t),m(t),ε(t)]`. `m(t)` includes who can enter the record, when they are measured, and which fields are withheld from disclosure for protection. `ε(t)` is used only when there is a corresponding error model. Unknowns must not all be called random noise. When conditions allow, numerical measurements can use additive error. Text and categorical records use their corresponding generation rules. Ω should preserve the distinction between the records and the judgments, so a change in observation rule is not mistaken for a change in the object.

The same set of records can correspond to several states or mechanisms. Let `Z(o)` denote the set of states still compatible with the record under the stated model and error bounds. If the answer to question q is the same in every state in `Z(o)`, the current material is sufficient for the question. A limited answer can be given without recovering every latent variable. If the values differ, the output should retain their range, any direction that can be identified, or the parts that genuinely remain unknown. Failure to detect a difference in a finite sample does not automatically establish that only one answer remains over the entire set.

The set also helps plan the next observation. First identify state distinctions that would change the current answer or the comparison of actions. Then check which feasible observations could distinguish them. More precision that leaves the answer unchanged need not help. A low-cost observation that narrows an important branch may be more valuable than expanding the whole variable table. Data collection remains subject to purpose, necessity, and protection. A need for identification does not confer permission to collect data.

### 11.3.2 Event Processing and State Continuation

Each input retains the time of the event, the time the record was obtained, and the time it entered the model. Material that arrives late can change what is now known about the past, but it cannot rewrite the information set available at the time. When correcting a source, append the link to the correction and recompute the states and paths that depend on that source. Retain local results that do not depend on it. When multiple sources recount one event, retain those sources and a single candidate identity for the event to avoid applying the same shock more than once. Events with similar descriptions must not be merged without verification.

State updating consists of two operations. An evidence update revises the judgment about `z(t)`. Advancing the mechanism estimates the change from `z(t)` to a subsequent state. A credible record that “work stopped yesterday” is an evidence update today. The stoppage itself belongs to yesterday’s real events. Rerunning a model and obtaining another response that work “may have stopped” adds only simulated material. Both kinds of update can change Ω, but their reasons and evidential statuses must be kept separate.

Concurrent events may be combined in any order only if the operations commute, shared resources are not in conflict, and neither changes the other’s conditions. If two actions compete for a resource, overwrite the same record, or change each other’s available action set, retain branches for the possible orders or specify an explicit rule for concurrency. Similar timestamps do not establish simultaneity. The order in which execution calls return does not by itself reveal the causal order of real events.

## 11.4 The Nine-Step Projection Loop

### 11.4.1 Step One. Freeze the Current State

Record the evidence cutoff, objects, variables, unknowns, disputes, and model version. Information obtained after the cutoff must not be fed back into the starting point. If it needs to be used, create a new version of the run.

### 11.4.2 Step Two. Identify Actors and Circles

Use Parts 9 and 10. If a circle has no grounds for object facts and the grouping gain claimed for it is not supported, retain it as a candidate grouping. If there is insufficient support for a personality hypothesis, retain it as a variable candidate. Do not turn candidates into entities merely to complete the graph.

### 11.4.3 Step Three. Separate Conditions in the Two Channels

List the relevant material conditions separately for the task. Also list the relevant experience–meaning conditions and declare the bridges needed between channels. If missing information from the other channel affects the current judgment, retain the unknown. Do not fill the gap by assuming symmetry. A channel genuinely irrelevant to the current task may be marked not applicable with a reason. Do not collect experiential material for the sake of completing the form. Suspend only propagation that depends on the missing channel. Independently established states and paths can still be carried forward.

### 11.4.4 Step Four. Declare Clocks, Thresholds, Capacity, and Delay

Identify each variable’s update clock. If a channel has capacity limits, delay, loss, saturation, reversal, or a recovery window, register them before propagation.

### 11.4.5 Step Five. Inject an Event or Action

A run may include multiple events and actions ordered by time, with their independent sources retained. First determine whether an action occurred, then record the actor, object, consequences, and authorization status separately. Lack of authorization cannot justify deleting a fact about what actually occurred. Plans, proposals, and simulated actions that have yet to occur retain their conditional status and must not be recorded as executed. If a model initiates or carries out a real-world action, valid authorization must be obtained separately. A recommendation for deliberation requires only that its evidence, normative premises, costs, gaps in authority, and conditions for revising the judgment be stated clearly.

### 11.4.6 Step Six. Propagate Along Declared Channels

First record directly confirmable state differences, then analyze return signals, indirect effects, and cross-circle cascades. An identified total effect of an intervention may enter a bounded judgment about the outcome. Unidentified parts of the internal mechanism remain unknown. The total effect must not be used to fill in missing edges of propagation. Explaining specific propagation requires corresponding material about channels and temporal order. Where there is only co-occurrence or correlation, retain common background, selection effects, and other competing explanations. Store state differences derived conditionally from the model separately from those observed in reality.

### 11.4.7 Step Seven. Generate Paths at Branch Points

Conditions, thresholds, actor choices, external disturbances, or uncertainty about mechanisms can all produce branching. Keep mutually exclusive paths separate. Retain parallel paths if they can occur at the same time. Create a merge node only when the conditions for merging are clear.

### 11.4.8 Step Eight. Register Signals, Residuals, and Variable Candidates

List early and contrary signals for each path. State differences that the current variables cannot explain enter the residuals. A residual can prompt an entry in the ledger of variable candidates, but cannot automatically supply a value in reality.

### 11.4.9 Step Nine. Write Back the Results

When actual results arrive, append which paths are supported, when results diverged from the projection, whether a simple baseline performed better, how well the projection was calibrated, and which mechanisms or variables should be downgraded, split, given revised boundaries, or retired. Write-back does not overwrite the original run.

## 11.5 Propagation, Delay, and Thresholds

An account of propagation must at least distinguish the arrival of a signal from an effective change of state. If information is seen but does not change a subsequent transition, record only arrival. If it changes beliefs without prompting action, record the change in experience–meaning state. Register a corresponding structural change only if the signal changes resources, behavior, rules, or relationships.

| Propagation Factor | Question for the Record | Possible Nonlinearity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Channel | What carrier conveys the impact from the source to the target? | Channel closure, filtering, or substitution |
| Capacity | How much can be carried per unit of time? | Saturation, congestion, and queues |
| Delay | When does it arrive, and when does it take effect? | Delayed feedback or a mistaken finding of no effect |
| Threshold | What conditions must be reached before the state changes? | Abrupt changes, hysteresis, and cascades |
| Loss | What is lost or changed in propagation? | Drift in meaning or dissipation of resources |
| Direction | Is the impact symmetric? | One-way dependence or offsetting effects in the opposite direction |
| Recovery | How does the object return to its previous state or move to a new one after a shock? | Resilience, cumulative damage, and lock-in |

When the same event reaches different circles through different channels, the effects can be opposite. Additional resources may reduce load in one circle but intensify competition in another. A public statement may repair external legitimacy but activate shame or distrust within the circle. A projection should record each channel separately. Net effects must not hide differences in distribution.

## 11.6 Feedback and Cross-Circle Cascades

Feedback requires a prior state or output to change a subsequent state, transition probability, or constraint through a return channel. A cascade requires a local change to trigger changes in other circles through membership overlap, bridging, shared resources, top-down institutional transmission, or network propagation. Both require evidence of temporal order and channels.

| Kind of Cascade | Typical Chain | What to Observe | Stopping Condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Membership | Overlapping members carry behavior, emotions, or information into another circle | Actual transmission as distinct from shared background | Members stop participating or information is not received |
| Resources | One circle takes up, releases, or reallocates resources | Accounting boundaries, conversion, and loss | Resources are isolated or an alternative source is available |
| Meaning | An interpretation of the event changes identity, legitimacy, or trust | Differences in interpretation across positions | The interpretation does not reach other circles or does not change their experience–meaning states. Effects on action or rules are checked separately |
| Institutions | An appeal, audit, or event changes rules that are then implemented at lower levels | Decisions, execution, and actual write-back | The rule is not implemented or is offset locally |
| Platforms | Recommendations, metrics, or public evaluations amplify propagation | Algorithms, selective visibility, and reflexivity | Exposure stops increasing or the audience does not respond |

The importance of a cascade does not automatically increase with scale. A small circle can have a large impact through a crucial bridge, while a large circle may have no effective propagation because its channels are blocked. A projection should focus on positions, channels, and thresholds. Membership counts alone are insufficient.

Two local effects can form a transmission chain only with shared conditions for response. Consider two subgroups of equal weight. In the first, the event changes the mediator, but the mediator does not affect the outcome. In the second, the mediator can affect the outcome, but the event cannot change the mediator. The mean effect on each edge is one-half, yet the effect of the event on the outcome across the entire chain can still be zero. This finite construction shows that the mean edge effects cannot be multiplied unconditionally even if the two studies use the same population. It is also necessary to know whether the successive effects occur in objects, states, and times that allow them to connect.

A cascade record should therefore check how the output of the preceding segment enters the next one, the scope of support for joint response, capacity and delay, and offsetting and parallel paths. A limited total effect already identified through an eligible comparison can be retained directly. What is added here is the responsibility to explain the specific propagation. It does not require explaining all micromechanisms before acknowledging any effect. When an edge has yet to be identified, retain the independently established upstream changes and terminal effects according to their own evidence.

## 11.7 A Graph of Branching Paths

A path node must include at least the parent node, condition set, triggering event or action, state differences, affected clocks, support status, a probability or ordering expression, early signals, contrary signals, the next node, and the reason for termination. The path graph should be a directed acyclic graph. If there is a feedback loop in reality, represent it with new nodes unfolded over time. A node must not be its own cause and effect within the same run.

Four kinds of uncertainty can produce branches: uncertainty about facts, uncertainty about mechanisms, actor choices, and exogenous disturbances. Different kinds should not be merged into a single vague probability. Give priority to verifying uncertain facts. Address mechanism uncertainty through observations that distinguish the alternatives. For uncertainty about choices, retain strategies and authorization. For exogenous disturbances, use scenario ranges and resilience checks.

| Source of Branching | Example | Appropriate Output | Inappropriate Output |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Facts | Did the event actually occur? | Conditional paths before verification | Choosing one version as fact |
| Mechanisms | Does silence come from fear or strategy? | Signals that distinguish the alternatives and parallel paths | Shutting down inquiry with a personality label |
| Choice | An actor cooperates, resists, or exits | Conditions and consequences of each choice | Claiming that free choices can be predicted precisely |
| Disturbance | Change in policy, prices, disasters, or technology | Stress scenarios and conditions for recovery | Attributing unmodeled residuals to fate |

## 11.8 The Ledger of Variable Candidates

Residuals that the model cannot explain must neither be deleted nor immediately named as real variables. The candidate ledger turns “what might we have missed?” into a testable task. Each candidate record must include at least the source residual, name, possible type, possible channel, observable implications, competing candidates, minimal test, privacy and risk, current status, evidence, and conditions for upgrading or rejecting it.

Candidate statuses include proposed, under test, supported candidate, rejected, and retired. Even with support, a candidate still has to enter the relevant object or variable contract. It must not directly become a stable personality, a supported circle, or a causal mechanism. Candidates proposed after results can improve the next model but cannot retroactively support the original prediction.

| Candidate Source | Permitted Action | What Must Be Avoided |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Residuals in the system | Propose several competing candidates and observations that distinguish them | Absorbing all errors into one mysterious variable |
| Account by a person concerned | Preserve the person’s position, the meaning of their account, and its verifiable parts | Automatically downgrading it to subjective noise or elevating it to a complete account of the facts |
| Recurrence across cases | Establish candidate mechanisms and limits of extrapolation | Treating similar narratives as universal laws |
| Model search | Pre-register the next round of testing | Selecting the best results for reporting after seeing them |
| AI suggestions | Generate and compare candidates. Form judgments and analytical recommendations when the evidence, permitted uses, and explicit normative premises allow | Letting AI output verify facts about reality or authorize action |

The framework can only identify residuals the current set of variables cannot explain, propose several distinguishable variable candidates, and design a minimal test with acceptable risk for each. It cannot decide on its own that a candidate is true in reality.

## 11.9 Scenarios, Counterfactuals, and Simulation

Scenario projection asks, “If these conditions hold, what paths emerge?” A counterfactual asks, “If an event or mechanism had been different, how might the outcome that actually occurred have changed?” Simulation unfolds specified rules and parameters. All three must be kept separate from descriptions of fact.

Counterfactuals especially require comparable conditions. When removing an event, also state which subsequent states, actors’ information, and circle relations change as a result. Do not remove an unwelcome cause while retaining all its consequences. The precision of a simulation cannot exceed what its inputs and evidence of mechanisms support. When parameters are unknown, use ranges, levels, or sensitivity analysis. Do not generate unsupported decimal places.

Counterfactuals must preserve the information boundaries at the time. Replacing an event or choice means reassessing the beliefs, resources, orders, and downstream behavior it affects. Do not remove an announcement while holding fixed the attendance and subsequent execution it brought about. Evidence for a short-range branch does not mean an alternative history decades later is already knowable. The actions of ordinary people, the discretion of frontline personnel, and organizational constraints can all enter the explanation. There is no need to assign causal percentages without a basis for identifying them. 〔Studies 03.03, 32.04, 32.08〕

The strength of a counterfactual conclusion depends on whether mechanisms still compatible with the evidence give the same answer under the altered operation. Two mechanisms can both produce observations of `X=M=Y`. In one, X changes Y through M. In the other, X changes M and Y separately. This co-movement alone cannot establish whether setting M directly would change Y. More observations of the same kind can improve the precision of the record but need not resolve this equivalence between mechanisms. A design that distinguishes the two mechanisms or independently grounded assumptions are needed. If independent materials already identify the total effect of a specified treatment within stated limits, retain that effect even if a particular mediator has yet to be identified.

Counterfactual analysis can therefore produce one answer, a set of feasible answers, or conditional bounds. Treat a direction as robust only when all compatible mechanisms support the same conclusion. If the conclusion reverses across plausible mechanisms, report the conditions under which it reverses and list the evidence needed to distinguish the mechanisms as the next observation. Historically, “removing a person from the post they came to occupy” and “that person having never been there” have different starting points and processes of adaptation. They cannot share the same counterfactual answer.

## 11.9.1 Multi-Order Recursive Projection of the Future

Advancing time over multiple steps, re-entry from a simulated state, and anticipatory reflexivity are three orthogonal dimensions. Advancing time over multiple steps carries a state forward in time within a single run. Simulation re-entry uses a simulated future generated in the first round as the starting point for a child run. Anticipatory reflexivity occurs when actors in the simulation form expectations about the more distant future or other people’s responses and change their current strategies. These three operations must not be conflated under the single term “recursion.”

Three orders are one default way to organize exploratory projection. The first unfolds direct consequences and competing paths. The second checks how those changes alter the available actions, resources, relationships, and adaptation. The third checks institutionalization, lock-in, reversal, and changes in conditions for generation. The orders mark responsibilities in projection. They do not amount to three points in time, natural stages, or levels of capability. Do not force recursion for static questions or tasks without a distinct question at the next order. Stop when conditions are insufficient.

The layer that generates possibilities may be bold. The state layer must be strict, the evidence layer must not raise its own status, and the action layer still needs separate authorization. Boldness can expand the space of plausible mechanisms, competing paths, and branches with low probability and severe consequences. It also helps produce clear judgments within the limits of the evidence. The strength of a judgment is supported only by evidence, conditions of applicability, and permitted uses. Tone or the number of generated possibilities cannot make it stronger.

### 11.9.2 Recursive Interfaces and Lineage

Before each re-entry, conduct a recasting audit. Record the current order, parent run, parent path, parent node, simulated time, the current order’s time window and the cumulative window, model version, inherited assumptions, new conditions, what is preserved, changed, collapsed, or omitted, new unknowns, effective variables for the target, residuals, identity across re-entry, the return path, and the reason for stopping. The simulated starting point must not be rewritten as present reality.

A child run must inherit the unresolved unknowns, losses, and residuals of its parent run. Selecting a hypothetical branch only narrows the conditions. It does not mean material from reality has eliminated the uncertainty. Increasing the number of draws may reduce computational error. It does not increase empirical support about outcomes in reality.

### 11.9.3 Branching, Merging, and Pruning

At each order, check whether there are substantive grounds for the main path, its strongest competitor, a path with low probability and severe consequences, and a route for residuals. Retain all conditions for paths that have grounds. If a category is inapplicable or a path of that kind cannot be constructed, explain why. Do not invent an event to fill a category. Branches must correspond to explainable differences in mechanisms, choices by actors, external disturbances, or unknowns of a specified kind. Retaining a path with severe consequences means only that it calls for investigation or preparation. It does not establish that its real-world probability is known.

Branches may be merged only when object identity is preserved, key states are equivalent within a predetermined tolerance, differences in history do not change subsequent transitions, and the available actions and constraints agree going forward. Pruning rules must be declared in advance. Preserve pruned branches, reasons for pruning, any possible probability mass, and the level of harm. Low probability alone cannot justify removing a path with severe consequences.

### 11.9.4 Early Stopping

Stop or downgrade if object identity or scale identity fails, key assumptions cannot be inherited, the transition mechanism falls outside its domain, the recursive interface lacks closure, or branches proliferate without signals to distinguish them. Also stop or downgrade if the results reverse across the board under small changes, residuals exceed the threshold for the intended use, a deeper order adds nothing relative to a shallower one and a simple baseline, the calibrated time window is exceeded, a boundary of rights or authorization is reached, or the third-order limit is reached. The stopping record must state what can still be said and what cannot.

## 11.10 Stopping Conditions for a Projection Run

Affected propositions and the paths that depend on them should be suspended or downgraded if a key object lacks grounds for object facts or a grouping-gain claim on which a path depends is not supported, the event’s factual status is unclear, a propagation channel is missing, a cross-scale mapping fails, or the number of paths grows without signals to distinguish them. The same rule applies if the model is extremely sensitive to small changes in parameters, privacy or safety risks exceed the value of the information, the projection is asked to generate a diagnosis, punishment, or authorization directly, or a simple baseline already performs as well or better. If a shared premise or the overall conditions for use fail, all related runs stop.

Stopping is part of the output. It must not be used to conceal failure. The stopping record should state where the projection stopped, what has been obtained, which unknowns prevent continuation, which next observation would be most informative, and what risk further projection would add.

## 11.11 An Example of a Cross-Circle Cascade

Continue with the public departure from a job in Part 10. The starting snapshot shows that the employee faces long working hours and the pressure of care work, team trust is declining, company management is concerned with reputation, the professional community provides outside opportunities, and public opinion on the platform has yet to form. The event is the employee publishing an account of their departure. Part of its content can be verified, and part is personal narrative.

Direct effects include the team receiving the information, the platform obtaining a text that can be circulated, and management facing pressure to respond. On the first path, the company makes only a public denial. In the short term, external public opinion may branch toward either fading or amplification. If members inside the company interpret the denial as suppression, team trust declines and further departures occur through bridges in the professional community. On the second path, the company acknowledges the problem and starts verifiable adjustments to resources and appeals. If these are carried out, working hours and processes change on the organizational clock. Trust may recover more slowly on the experience–meaning clock. On the third path, changes in family care conditions ease the employee’s pressure after leaving. This does not directly change the team’s state unless the employee continues to bridge information or resources.

Early signals can include the number of internal appeals, records of hours actually worked, members’ public statements, and changes in hiring and departures. Contrary signals can include no actual change in resources after a policy is announced, increasing internal silence despite declining external exposure, or the professional community failing to form a bridge. Residuals may come from the states of key managers, the platform’s recommendation mechanism, or unobserved legal constraints. These enter only the ledger of variable candidates.

The projection still cannot say which path the company “should” take. It provides conditional consequences and signals that distinguish the paths. Forecast registration, comparison of options, normative premises, and authorization are addressed in the next Part.

## 11.12 Outputs of This Part

A qualifying projection output should include a frozen joint snapshot, event type and evidence, mechanism and parameter assumptions, update steps, and a path graph. For every path, it should record conditions, clocks, support status, and early and contrary signals. It should also include residuals and variable candidates, stopping conditions, a plan for writing back results, and an explicit statement separating prediction and action.

Projection can improve structured preparation for what follows, but it remains conditional. Without registration before results, a simple baseline, calibration, and continuing write-back, it cannot support a claim that foresight capability has improved. Without normative grounds and authorization, it cannot support a claim that an action should be carried out. An analysis based on explicit normative premises can recommend an option, reject it, or recommend deferral. None of these judgments establishes that the option has been authorized for execution. For each such judgment, its grounds, costs, opposing case, and conditions for revision must be stated in full.

## 11.13 Action Choice and Updating Conditions

For dynamic questions involving actors, having a model evaluate a set of static options is insufficient. At each step, first use the current state to identify actions actually available, actions ruled out, and the source of the restrictions. Then analyze how an actor might choose given their information, resources, relationships, habits, and goals. Capability, belief, willingness, and actual action must be kept separate. Being able to act does not mean the actor will act. Stating an intention does not mean it has been carried out. An analyst’s recommendation does not amount to adoption by the actor.

For each choice with a basis in the material, specify which mechanism it triggers, which states change first, who is affected through which interfaces, and how these changes alter the set of actions available at the next step. State differences must be expressed in concrete terms such as resources, rules, relationships, beliefs, or feasible actions. Feeding the same materials to another model and generating another narrative cannot substitute for updating a state. If the material does not determine the choice, retain several conditional paths. Do not decide for the actor just to continue the projection.

Later projection starts from explicitly stated conditional state differences and inherits unresolved unknowns and restrictions. When real feedback arrives, first verify its source and event status. Then compare the original path, competing paths, and the simple baseline. Distinguish factual errors, mechanism errors, different choices of action, deviations in execution, and external changes. The corrected result should change the states, parameters, scope of mechanisms, or paths used in the next round. Keeping only the text of the feedback while retaining the same choice rule does not warrant claiming that the model has learned.

[^tn-circle-12]: Translator's note: Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, or bridged.

[^tn-contract-12]: Translator's note: A contract here is an analytical agreement registered and frozen before results, akin to pre-registration.

<a id="part_12"></a>

# Part 12 Conditional Foresight and Bounded Choice

Projection unfolds conditions and paths. Conditional foresight goes further by making judgments about the future that can fail. Bounded choice compares what can be done, how to do it, when to stop, and when to refrain within the bounds of values, protection, and authorization. This Part places foresight and choice together to form a closed loop of work. Their separate contracts[^tn-contract-13] prevent “a consequence was predicted” from being taken to mean “there is therefore authority to make others take on an action.” Comparing and recommending options are analytical outputs and can be completed when normative premises and constraints are clear. Real-world adoption and execution must separately undergo the applicable protection, review, and authorization procedures.

## 12.1 Keeping the Four Outputs Separate

Explanation, projection, conditional foresight, and bounded choice must be labeled separately.

| Output | Core Question | Required Inputs | Output Ceiling |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Explanation | What has happened, and why might it have happened? | Object, scale, evidence, mechanism, and counterexamples | An account of the mechanism within stated limits |
| Projection | Which paths could unfold after a condition or event? | Frozen snapshot, update rules, clocks, and branches | Conditional paths and signals that distinguish them |
| Conditional foresight | For the stated target and horizon, which path is better supported as an expectation? | Registration before results, a simple baseline, metrics, and write-back | A judgment that can be calibrated and can fail |
| Bounded choice | Compare and recommend options under explicit normative premises.
