Real-world adoption separately undergoes protection, review, and authorization | Analysis requires normative premises, evidence, options, costs, and the people affected. Real-world adoption also requires N, PF, J, O, and C12 | A refutable analytical recommendation or an authorized record of bounded choice, with their statuses kept separate |

Explanation can generate candidates for projection, but it cannot automatically establish what will happen. Projection can generate candidate forecasts, but it cannot count every path as a success. Foresight can inform comparisons between options, but it cannot automatically generate values or authority. Even a bounded choice that passes its checks does not guarantee successful execution in reality. Stopping, rollback, and outcome write-back are still required.

## 12.2 Conditional Forecast Registration

Each forecast is frozen after the input cutoff and before the results appear. The minimum fields include forecast ID, model version, target, horizon, scope of objects and circles[^tn-circle-13], baseline time, input cutoff, simple baseline, candidate mechanisms, set of paths, probability or ranking expression, calibration plan, decision threshold, early signals, contrary signals, suspension conditions, retirement conditions, outcome write-back, registration time, and evidence references. Include the decision threshold when it is used for the corresponding decision. Otherwise mark it not applicable.

The target must be decidable. For example, “the relationship will improve” must specify which observables change within a stated window, who observes them, what threshold applies, and what outcomes count as unresolved. The horizon can be a window. There is no need to fabricate a precise date. The scope of the objects and circles must be frozen to avoid narrowing it to the successful subgroup after results appear.

| Registration Item | Minimum Question | Bias It Prevents |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Target | When the horizon ends, how will we know whether it occurred, did not occur, or cannot be determined? | Vague success criteria |
| Horizon | When does the window start and end, and when does input collection stop? | Waiting indefinitely |
| Simple baseline | What is the comparison prediction without multi-circle mechanisms? | Letting a complex story win by its own standards |
| Paths | Which paths are mutually exclusive, parallel, or incomparable? | Post hoc selection |
| Expression | Probability, interval, level, or direction alone? | False precision |
| Signals | What would strengthen or weaken support for a path? | Looking only for supporting evidence |
| Write-back | What is the source of the outcome, and when is it appended? | Letting failures disappear |

### 12.2.1 Limits of Local Predictability

Every predictability claim must specify its object, state region, scale and circles, time window, allowable error, available information, computing and observation resources, model version, baseline, abstention conditions, and failure conditions. This framework cannot directly answer whether the world as a whole is predictable. It can test only whether a particular task gains a limited increment within explicit bounds.

Classifications, levels, intervals, and probabilities carry different responsibilities for measurement and expression. They do not form a ladder of precision that every task has to climb. The chosen form of expression must be supported by the target, units, generation method, identification conditions, and calibration status. If there is no basis even for a qualitative direction, retain it as unknown. Direction is not the default when probability is unavailable. For known probabilities in formal models and for empirical probabilities, state the assumptions and limits of extrapolation separately. Text patterns and AI must not be used to invent real-world parameters. When citing an existing mathematical or physical theory, also register its original object, assumptions, mathematical limits or scaling laws, error, domain of validity, object mapping, counterexamples, and exit conditions. Borrowing the form of its equations alone is insufficient.

### 12.2.2 Evaluation of Recursive Foresight by Order

Freeze and evaluate the first, second, and third orders separately. They must not be combined into a single overall hit rate. Each order must be compared with state continuation without further recursion, a direct longer-horizon judgment, and a shallower order followed by simple continuation. If going to the third order adds only narrative without stable out-of-sample gains, it may be retained as an exploratory scenario. It does not qualify as evidence of foresight capability.

Without new external material, the source lineage of every child run still ends at the same frozen starting point. Post-processing can add reasoning within the model and reveal sensitivity and hidden consequences. It cannot manufacture evidence about reality. When actual intermediate outcomes arrive, a new frozen run should be created and compared with the original recursive paths. The outcomes must not be fed back into the old paths.

## 12.3 Simple Baselines and Incremental Capability

A multi-circle model claimed to improve foresight must be compared with simple models suited to the task and with existing methods in the domain under the same information, cutoff, target, and evaluation rules. Make the comparison materials, inference budgets, and opportunities for tuning as comparable as possible. State separately what is contributed by additional information, additional computation, and additional structural assumptions. A simple baseline need not be artificially enlarged to match the size of the complex model. Predictive systems generally require independent testing or out-of-sample review. If neither can be obtained, explicitly retain the status that the capability has yet to be established.

Common baselines include continuing the current trend, carrying forward the most recent state, using historical frequencies, using only the actor state, using only the primary circle state, and using an existing model in the domain. Comparisons should use the same target, horizon, data cutoff, and evaluation metrics. If a complex model improves explanation but does not improve prediction, record the outcome as “a gain in explanation is present, and a gain in foresight is not supported.”

Model complexity also has costs. More variables increase missing data, measurement error, privacy risk, overfitting, and maintenance debt. If a multi-circle model works in only a few cases, its domain of application should be restricted. If gains disappear over time, it should be downgraded or retired. If the simple baseline performs equally well, it should be preferred.

## 12.4 Probability, Levels, and Time Windows

Where the evidence permits, one may give a probability or probability interval and state the model, prior, data, and grounds for calibration. When there is insufficient basis for a probability, one may give levels or compare paths to the extent supported by evidence. Sample size alone does not determine the type of output. When even the direction or ordering cannot be identified, explicitly retain it as unknown and identify observations that could distinguish the alternatives. Giving only the direction does not waive evidential responsibility. The strength of the expression depends on the evidence and use, independently of how much certainty the user wants.

| Evidential Status | Appropriate Expression | What Must Accompany It |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A probability model or empirical estimate with clear grounds and a decidable target | Probability or probability interval | Method of generation, prior if applicable, data, limits of applicability, and calibration status |
| Insufficient basis for probability, but grounds for path levels or ordering | Levels of support or an ordering | Grounds, incomparable items, and contrary signals |
| The direction can be identified, or it remains unknown | A supported conditional direction, or an explicit unknown | Identification conditions, trigger points, minimal observation, and stopping conditions |
| Target or object is unstable | Withhold the forecast | The contract that must be redefined |

Time windows should match mechanism clocks. Immediate reactions can use a short window. Organizational or institutional changes require a longer one. Compressing a long-term mechanism into a short period can lead to a mistaken finding of no effect. Extrapolating a short-term signal indefinitely can lead to a mistaken finding of a trend. When several clocks coexist, separate horizons may be registered for different intermediate outcomes.

Real-world probabilities, actors’ subjective beliefs, decision weights, a model’s ordering of paths, and the allocation of computation at the next step are distinct quantities. A real-world probability must specify the target event and the basis for estimation or calibration. For a subjective belief, state who is judging, what information they have, and how they reached the judgment. Decision weights are interpreted according to the descriptive model or normative premises in use. Computational priorities specify why further investigation is worthwhile. Frequencies of branches obtained by taking more draws from a model must not be treated directly as real-world probabilities. The path most worth investigating or most hoped for must not be described as the most likely.

When probabilities cannot be determined reliably, a path may be investigated first because its consequences are severe, the response window is short, or it is easy to distinguish. Disclose the reason for the priority and the budget for investigating it, and retain the probability as unknown. Percentages must not be invented to establish an ordering. An action comparison still has to state normative objectives, distributional consequences, and feasible choices separately. An ordering by probability does not directly determine the order of recommendations.

Probabilities and levels have different meanings. A model’s conditional probability under given assumptions, uncertainty in an individual judgment, empirical calibration in a long series of repeated tasks, and normative priority must not use one number as though they referred to the same thing. A posterior can be obtained from a small sample and an explicit prior, but its real-world applicability still depends on the model. A large sample cannot repair an incorrect definition of what is observed. Without evidence, even the direction can remain unknown. Reducing the claim to direction alone does not automatically make it reliable. 〔Studies 03.04, 07.07〕

## 12.5 Calibration and Evaluation

Foresight capability is established by results registered over time. It does not follow from a style that sounds accurate. Where applicable, evaluate probability tasks with the Brier score, log loss, calibration curves, resolution, and interval coverage. Use pre-registered ranking metrics for ranking tasks. Use precision, recall, or domain metrics for event detection. Use window coverage and lead time for predictions of timing.

Every metric must match the actual use. If low-probability events with severe harms call for caution, evaluation cannot rely only on overall accuracy. A false positive costs something different when the output prompts further observation than when it is used to compel action. Good metrics establish neither that the model’s explanation is correct nor that action is justified.

Once early warning is in use, the object of evaluation must specify the response regime. Is the task to predict actual events under an existing regime, or to ask separately what issuing a warning or taking action changed? Retain records of reach, execution, entries and exits of the objects, and how the data were produced, where these affect evaluation. When the regime changes, state again which outcomes are still comparable. The absence of an observed event cannot automatically be recorded as a false positive or taken to establish successful protection. A claim about harm avoided separately requires a causal comparison suited to that claim. Forecasts already defined under the current response regime can still be evaluated against the original target. There is no need to turn every prediction task into a study of intervention effects. The original target, horizon, and success rules must not be rewritten after the fact because a response took place (Perdomo et al., 2020). See also §2.4 and §12.6.

| Evaluation Dimension | Question | Action After Failure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Calibration | Of events assigned 70%, do roughly 70% occur over the long run? | Adjust the expression or downgrade probability outputs |
| Resolution | Can the model distinguish different risks or paths? | Remove variables that make no contribution and return to the baseline |
| Coverage | Do intervals or time windows cover the actual outcomes? | Widen intervals or revise the clocks |
| Gain over baseline | Does it outperform the simple model? | Stop making foresight claims for the complex model |
| Stability | Does performance hold across times, locations, and boundaries? | Restrict the domain of application or retire the model |
| Distribution of error | At which positions do persistent underestimates or false positives occur? | Review by people affected, remediation, and stronger protection |

Three parallel questions can fix the terms of the evaluation.

| Question | What Sufficient Material Can Answer | What Must Be Retained |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How did the original prediction perform on the frozen target? | Compute loss, classification outcomes, or coverage using actual labels defined on the same basis | A single case score does not establish long-run calibration. Do not enter zero for missing labels |
| How much did publication or action change the outcome? | Give a limited effect estimate based on a causal comparison suited to the question | An observed absence of the event cannot by itself distinguish initially low risk from successful prevention |
| Is it appropriate to adopt this prediction and response? | Compare protection, false rejections, delay, distribution, and cost under explicit normative premises | Neither predictive accuracy nor a positive effect by itself confers permission |

For example, suppose a forecast registered in advance assigns an event probability of 0.8 under the current response regime, and the complete label at the end of the horizon is 0. The Brier loss for that case is 0.64. If the threshold for binary classification was set in advance at 0.5, a false positive may also be recorded for that target. This limited calculation establishes neither the risk without action nor that the response was ineffective. If monitoring was offline and no label was obtained, the same calculation cannot be used. Complete labels for people who were approved or received services can directly evaluate only the corresponding population. Extending the evaluation to people who were rejected, were not reached, or exited requires labels for them or well-grounded methods of identification and bounding.

After a change in the response regime, first check whether the original target is still the same question. If the target is retained, report the performance change as observed. If the target shifts to a different response regime or population, create a new registration and retain the old results. Reflexivity must not be used to cancel a score that can be computed. One successful response must not be used to rescue all incorrect predictions after the fact.

## 12.6 Early Signals, Contrary Signals, and Trigger Points

An early signal is an observable change that the path’s mechanism should produce first. A smaller version of the outcome does not suffice. A contrary signal indicates that a key condition of the path is failing or that a competing path is gaining support. Both must be registered before results to avoid collecting only supporting material.

Trigger points connect foresight to review. Observing a signal calls for reassessing the object, path, probability, normative grounds, and authorization. It does not automatically trigger action. High-risk cases should also have veto triggers, such as a breach of the protection floor, inability of key affected people to exit, distortion of data sources, or deteriorating model calibration.

Use completion of a feasible action as the endpoint when comparing clocks. Detecting the signal, verifying it, publishing the warning, reaching people, and understanding the warning each involve a delay. Gathering resources takes time, as can carrying out a transfer or waiting for a measure to take effect. Earlier algorithmic detection does not guarantee that the last person affected can receive protection before harm occurs. If no harm follows an alert, use the frozen target and labeling conditions to decide whether an operational false positive may be registered. Identify separately whether a successful response prevented harm. Keep the denominators separate for the operational false positive rate, the proportion of false alerts among alerts, and the missed detection rate. 〔Studies 31.05〕

## 12.7 Failure Semantics for Forecasts

When the horizon ends, the result can be supported, unsupported, unresolved, target invalid, or not evaluable. Unresolved is the state of insufficient evidence specified by the contract. It is no compromise between success and failure. A result that cannot be evaluated must state what went wrong with the data, object, horizon, or execution. An invalid target means the object of the prediction or its identity criterion has undergone conversion. It must not be counted as a hit.

Changing the target, horizon, or scope of circles after results, or merging several paths into “it happened to some extent,” cannot preserve the original forecast’s eligibility to count as a success. A new interpretation can be recorded in a new version. The old record remains.

## 12.8 The Bridge From Foresight to Bounded Choice

Foresight outputs answer only questions about conditional consequences. An analytical recommendation requires explicit normative premises, evidence, affected positions, options, costs and benefits, and conditions for revising the judgment. A recommendation does not confer permission to execute. Before real-world adoption, the record must also include explicit normative premises, the choosing subject, jurisdiction, affected positions, rights floors, options, the distribution of costs and benefits, authorization, stopping, appeal, rollback, and remedy. N, PF, J, O, and C12 still apply. Prediction provides no shortcut.

Prediction cannot automatically confer authorization. Even an extremely probable path does not show that anyone has authority to compel others to bear the cost of avoiding it. Even the highest average benefit does not show that the rights of positions in the minority may be ignored. Even if the model recommends taking no action, continuing harm and responsibility arising from inaction still require review.

## 12.9 The Option Set Must Include Action and No Action

When generating options, check the applicability of each of six categories: maintaining the status quo, acting proactively, delaying action, taking a small exploratory action, exiting or transferring, and explicitly taking no action. For each applicable category, develop genuine and comparable options. Explain why any category does not apply. Do not invent options to meet a quota. If a choice is requested, do not remove the explicit no-action baseline or combine it with maintaining the status quo. Maintaining the status quo may require ongoing resources and execution. Taking no action may allow existing conditions to change on their own. Specific tasks may add options. Tasks that do not request a choice need not produce a list of actions.

| Option Type | Main Use | Risks That Must Be Checked |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Maintain the status quo | Protect existing stability and uptake arrangements | Hidden costs, continuing harm, and lock-in |
| Proactive action | Change a path or its conditions quickly | Authority, irreversibility, and cross-circle spillovers |
| Delayed action | Wait for information, procedure, or the right time | Harm and lost opportunities from delay itself |
| Exploratory action | Obtain information through a small reversible step | Who bears the costs of the trial and whether it can actually be rolled back |
| Exit or transfer | End an unsuitable object or relationship | People unable to exit, responsibilities, and preservation of history |
| No action | Avoid overstepping authority or taking high-risk action | Bias toward the default, continuing consequences, and responsibility for inaction |

Taking no action does not mean that nothing happens. Other actors, circles, resources, and clocks are still changing. The no-action option must have a baseline time, expected path, observation plan, conditions for reopening the decision, and a responsible party.

## 12.10 A Common Card for Comparing Options

Compare each option against the same baseline. Record at least the option description, forecast reference, normative premises, people affected, rights floors, expected path, worst acceptable outcome, cross-circle spillovers, distribution of costs and benefits, value of information, risk of lock-in, reversibility, resource costs, implementer, authorization, stopping, rollback, and remedy.

| Dimension of Comparison | Question | What Cannot Substitute |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Target and path | Which path is the option intended to change? | An abstract promise to “make things better” |
| Protection floor | Who might suffer unacceptable harm? | Average benefit |
| Distribution | Who benefits, who pays, and when? | The aggregate net value |
| Cross-circle spillovers | Does local improvement externalize costs? | Metrics from the primary circle |
| Value of information | Can the option distinguish mechanisms or paths? | The dramatic character of the action itself |
| Reversibility and lock-in | If it goes wrong, can it be stopped, reversed, or remedied? | Verbal promises |
| Permissions | Who may decide, execute, and appeal? | A model’s suggestion or a job title |
| No action | What path will unfold if nothing is done? | An assumption of zero cost |

If options cannot be compared on a single scale, retain the outcomes on multiple dimensions and disclose the conflicts. Do not force them into a sum. Conflicts among value premises should be handled by qualified subjects and procedures. The model should fully show where conflicts arise and what their consequences are. It may offer a preference and reasons for deliberation under openly stated normative premises. It cannot make a binding final decision in place of a qualified subject.

The value of information can be written as a limited formal object. Let a denote the action, θ the state, Z the information, and U the utility accepted in advance. Then `EVSI=E_Z[max_a E(U|Z)]−max_a E(U)`. EVSI is nonnegative when Z is free to obtain and can be ignored, and when the action set and objective remain unchanged. Actual investigation has costs in time, privacy, and resources. It may miss a window or change the available actions. Its net value should be counted separately. Two clues may change the action only if both are obtained. The value of understanding can exist independently and need not all be converted into this quantity for decision-making. 〔Studies 07.08, formal statement of conditions〕

### 12.10.1 From Identifiable Answers to Feasible Choices

A question can have a sufficient answer while action remains infeasible. There can also be a commonly acceptable action before all states have been identified. For each state z compatible with the available material, let `A_ok(z)` denote the actions acceptable under the disclosed minimum protections, resources, and valid permissions. In a finite, single-round deterministic choice, if the intersection `∩ A_ok(z)` is nonempty, the common options can be compared without first distinguishing all states. This gives one condition under which bounded action is possible while unknowns remain. If the intersection is empty, “there is a solution for every state” is insufficient to establish that the right one can be chosen now. Check whether obtaining more evidence, waiting, narrowing the task, or taking a protective action with independent grounds could change the conditions.

The intersection concerns only the credible states listed and the explicit constraints. It does not guarantee that unknown states will never arise. Risk probabilities, costs, or normative tolerance can support other choice rules, but they must be stated explicitly. The worst-case criterion must not be concealed as the only rational one. Permissible randomized or sequential strategies must also be analyzed separately for their respective objectives. The single-round condition above does not rule them out.

### 12.10.2 Joint Feasibility of Multiple Options

When several actions are feasible individually, also check whether implementing them at the same time would draw on the same capacity, budget, staff, and stopping channels. If two units of service must both be completed in the current period and it has capacity for only one unit, each is feasible on its own but they are infeasible together. Added capacity next period cannot recover a deadline already missed in this period. Adding capacity in the same period or adjusting the deadline with adequate grounds and authorization can yield a new feasible option. It must not be used to rewrite the old task as completed.

Joint comparison should therefore account for resource use, task dependencies, and key deadlines together so different departments do not commit the same resource twice. Parallel work shortens waiting only when conditions allow. Where investigation, authorization, implementation, and taking effect depend on one another in sequence, they must still follow that order. Treating completion of the first node as proof that the last affected person is already protected misjudges both timing and coverage.

## 12.11 Action Ceilings and Trials to Obtain Information

Evidence, reversibility, permissions, and protection together determine the action ceiling. When evidence is weak, risk high, action irreversible, or permissions insufficient, an appropriate output may be to observe first, narrow the action, improve the information, protect people affected, initiate an appeal, or take no action for now.

A trial to obtain information still carries responsibility, however small the action. It must specify the trial object, people affected, minimum scope, reversibility, stopping, data uses, and remedy. Those in low-power positions must not be used as material for model calibration. Participation must not be described as voluntary where there is no genuine ability to exit.

| Combination of Conditions | Example of the Permitted Ceiling |
| --- | --- |
| Weak evidence, low harm, reversibility, and clear authorization | Small-scale observation or exploratory action |
| Moderate evidence, manageable harm, and a feasible rollback | Phased action with a stringent stopping check |
| Strong evidence but insufficient permissions | Submit to the authorized subject and do not execute independently |
| Strong evidence, but the action is irreversible and protection requirements are unmet | Suspend and complete the procedures and protections |
| Urgent safety threat | Take only the minimum protective action under external emergency authorization, and review it as soon as possible |

## 12.12 Outcome Write-Back and Model Learning

Writing back outcomes for foresight and choices is append-only. The record includes the time of outcome observation, source, actual outcome, match to paths, calibration metrics, comparison with the simple baseline, unexpected effects, review at affected positions, model updates, downgraded assertions, retired variables or paths, and the next review.

Action outcomes must not be evaluated only against the main target. Also check side effects, cross-circle spillovers, cost distribution, appeals, people unable to exit, and long-term clocks. One success does not establish that a mechanism holds universally. One failure may be due to a deviation in execution. Use the frozen contract to distinguish evidence about the mechanism from deviations in execution. Explanations about execution must not be used arbitrarily to rescue the model.

## 12.13 A Complete Example of Foresight and Choice

Continue with the public departure from a job. A conditional forecast could set the target as “whether voluntary turnover in the team over the next three months is clearly above the historical baseline and the baseline for similar teams,” with an input cutoff one week after the public statement. The simple baseline uses historical departure rates and trends in working hours. The multi-circle model adds team trust, management’s response, bridging by the professional community, family care, and platform exposure. Possible paths include public attention fading while internal problems persist, institutional adjustments followed by gradual repair, and external bridging that triggers departures in succession.

Early signals include actual changes in working hours, handling of appeals, statements by members, hiring, and outside opportunities. Contrary signals include a policy being announced without execution, or public quiet accompanied by increasing silence internally. After three months, compare the multi-circle model with the simple baseline. Do not declare success by picking an intermediate signal that happened to match.

Bounded choice should compare at least maintaining the status quo, responding only through public relations, immediately adjusting resources, starting with a reversible pilot for working hours and appeals, delaying major reorganization to gather information, allowing team members to transfer, and taking no structural action for now while strengthening protection and observation. Each option requires checking permissions, people unable to exit, the distribution of costs, reputational effects across circles and effects on families, stopping, and remedy. The model can show which options are more likely to change the path under which conditions. The final choice still depends on normative premises, lawful authorization, and procedures for the people affected. Under these conditions, the model should also state its current analytical recommendation, why other options were not selected, the costs, and the conditions for revising the judgment. A recommendation does not change the status of execution permission that has yet to be obtained.

## 12.14 When to Choose Not to Act

Taking no action can be prudent or evasive. Taking no action for now or limiting activity to protective observation can be warranted when the object and facts are unclear, an action is irreversible and evidence insufficient, permissions are missing, or the protection floor is unmet. It can also be warranted when the proposed action is expected to shift costs to people unable to exit, neither the simple baseline nor the complex model can distinguish the paths, or external intervention could interrupt an ongoing recovery.

Taking no action requires stricter review when harm is ongoing, inaction reinforces entrenched advantages, the responsible subject has a clear statutory duty or commitment, or people in low-power positions cannot exit on their own. The same applies when delay would create irreversible lock-in or when “waiting for more evidence” is used to defer indefinitely. Even without the main action, minimum protection, disclosure, appeal channels, or temporary uptake may be needed.

## 12.15 Where This Part Stops

The conditional foresight layer may publish judgments with a target, horizon, baseline, signals, calibration, and write-back. When norms, protection, and authorization are all clear, the bounded choice layer can compare action, delay, exploration, exit, and no action, and provide a conditional, withdrawable choice record. Even when execution has not been authorized, an analytical recommendation can still be completed within what the evidence and explicit normative premises allow. It must fully explain the options, opposing arguments, distributional consequences, and conditions for revising the judgment. Its status for real-world adoption must be recorded separately.

These layers cannot guarantee the future, eliminate conflicts of value, replace law, professional norms, participation by people affected, or real-world authorization, or turn model accuracy into a qualification to rule. Any use that exceeds these boundaries should be blocked by the tool gate in Part 13 and governed, suspended, or retired under Part 16.

## 12.16 Format for Publication to Readers

When publishing externally, deliver the full analysis by default. Open with the current judgment and its scope, then develop the conditions, evidence, and uncertainty in full. The main text should retain the fields that apply to the current kind of output, including the current cutoff, target and horizon, scope of objects and circles, simple baseline, all substantive path comparisons already performed, the conditions for each path to hold, early and contrary signals, strength of expression, suspension, and the next review. Substantive analysis must not be omitted on the grounds that it does not change the main conclusion, belongs to a secondary branch, or is not a decisive reason. Conditions must not be hidden in footnotes, and “the model thinks” must not stand in for evidential responsibility. Shorten the visible answer only when the user explicitly asks for a brief reply or specifies a shorter length, while retaining the full analysis. If the analysis exceeds the capacity of one delivery, deliver it section by section and provide the complete file, stating what has yet to be delivered. Summaries, links to attachments, and coverage markers cannot substitute for the argument in the main text.

An analysis of bounded choice should state explicitly that it has entered the normative layer. It should list the value premises, deciding subject, affected positions identified and the limits of identification, protection floor, available options, distributional consequences of action and no action, authorization status, action ceiling, stopping, rollback, and remedy in full. The current analytical recommendation may come first, followed by the complete reasons for accepting and ruling out each option, costs, objections, and conditions for revising the judgment. If authorization has yet to be obtained, state clearly that the recommendation is for deliberation and that execution in reality has yet to be authorized. An imperative must not be used to make a recommendation look like a decision that can be executed. Lack of execution permission must not reduce an analytical choice with adequate grounds to a list of questions.

| Publication Status | Permitted Wording | What Must Be Avoided |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Explanation only | “Current evidence supports mechanism A and leaves B open.” | “Therefore, the next step must occur.” |
| Projection only | “If X continues and Y occurs, path P receives support.” | “The model’s prediction of P is settled.” |
| Registered forecast | “Within horizon T, P currently takes priority over Q. See contrary signal S.” | Hiding the baseline, horizon, or failure conditions |
| Normative checks not yet passed | “An analytical recommendation can be made under explicit premises. Real-world execution still requires N, PF, J, and O.” | “Authorization has yet to be obtained, but execution can begin immediately.” |
| Externally authorized | “The authorization record permits the subject to execute within the ceiling and stop under the stated conditions.” | Attributing authorization to the model |

Interpretation after publication is also subject to governance. If readers mistake conditional foresight for a certain prophecy, a candidate personality hypothesis for a fact, or an option comparison for an order, the publisher should correct the misunderstanding without exploiting it to increase the publication’s impact. Append any update as a new version and keep the original title, probabilities, horizon, and failure record traceable.

[^tn-contract-13]: Translator's note: A contract here is an analytical agreement registered and frozen before results, akin to pre-registration.

[^tn-circle-13]: Translator's note: Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, or bridged.

<a id="part_13"></a>

# Part 13 Interfaces and Tools

This Part converts the objects, propositions, scales, mechanisms, actors, multi-circle joint states, and dynamic projection requirements defined in the preceding 12 Parts into limited judgments that can be observed, questioned, reviewed, and withdrawn. It adds no facts about the world. Completing a form does not establish a fact. A D0 candidate object record and a nine-axis scale record must exist before the tool workflow begins. If the object, boundary, scale, time window, identity criterion, or intended use changes, existing judgments must not simply be carried over.

The tool workflow has two levels. The seven gates are the only main workflow. The five gates and 13 steps elaborate the diagnosis[^tn-diagnosis-14]. The seven gates determine whether a judgment can proceed to the next level. In complex cases the five gates and 13 steps spell out the material that must be submitted. They do not issue a separate “pass” that competes with the seven gates or produce a second set of workflow statuses.

The tool layer confers no authorization. Even when the workflow, evidence, and strong-judgment kit are all complete, the outputs are limited to descriptions, candidate mechanisms, comparative judgments, strong-judgment candidates, or requirements for action. Real-world action still requires a separate structured selection record, the 10-component C12 bridge, one and the same valid atomic J authorization, and O1–O4. A tool output cannot substitute for any of them.

The following workflow governs structured judgments formed under this framework and their handoff. Reading, checking against the original text, and clarifying a low-impact question can use concise records suited to the task. Skipping the full tool workflow is no basis for declaring that established facts in the domain do not exist. Formal diagnosis, foresight, or high-impact use requires the corresponding full contract[^tn-contract-14]. Renaming an output cannot reduce the responsibilities it carries.

## 13.1 Four Conversions

Each conversion adds a responsibility for recording. Replacing terms from the theory with labels for real-world situations does not accomplish this. If any step fails, the outputs that depend on it stop before that step. A later step’s completed fields cannot make up for the failure. Other independent judgments are reviewed separately on their own grounds and intended uses.

| ID | Conversion | Required Result | Where to Stop If It Fails |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IT1 | Structural variables to observable signals | A record of signals that specifies the object, scale, observation position, time window, measurement protocol, and missing-data status | A conceptual mapping or a gap in the material |
| IT2 | Observable signals to strength of evidence | An evidence record that separates sources, observations, comparison conditions, coverage, counterexamples, alternative explanations, and uncertainty | Ungraded material or a hypothesis to test |
| IT3 | Strength of evidence to level of judgment | A judgment with a proposition contract, limits of applicability, contrary conditions, falsification conditions, and withdrawal conditions | A candidate explanation or an observational assessment |
| IT4 | Level of judgment to requirements and ceiling for action | A list of affected positions, the protection floor, prohibited actions, stopping, appeal, rollback, and outstanding N/J/O/C12 requirements | Description, a need for more evidence, or a requirement to take no action |

IT1 prevents a narrative about structure from being treated as an observed fact. IT2 prevents citations, materials, or declarations of position from being treated directly as evidence. IT3 prevents correlation, analogy, or one narrative of how a mechanism works from being elevated to a conclusion. IT4 prevents a leap from a factual judgment to action. Passing all four conversions leaves authorization status unchanged.

If any IT conversion fails, dependent later conversions may only be marked `not_run`. The propositions, comparative judgments, strong-judgment candidates, and requirements for action that they support must be stopped or downgraded. Downstream conclusions that share the same gap must not regain their ceiling through renaming, splitting, or opening another branch. A later `not_run` only preserves the chain of failure. Completing the kit cannot repair the break. A judgment may retain its original standing if it does not depend on the failed step, has independent grounds, and its use meets its own requirements. If the gap affects the overall source status, a shared factual premise, the object boundary, or lawful conditions for use of the entire task, stop or downgrade all dependent outputs together. If a gate required for high-impact adoption fails, calling the same judgment an ordinary opinion does not allow it to retain its original use.

## 13.2 Using the Universal Primitives and Human Variable Interfaces (11+11)

HV01–HV11 in Part 7 are the 11 interfaces for the human structured world. U01–U11 in Part 3 are the universal structural primitives. The tool layer can use both sets in the same case. Matching numbers, equal counts, or similar questions do not establish one-to-one equivalence.

At minimum, record the universal or human interface ID, effective object, nine-axis scale, selected support route, instance evidence, alternative explanations, missing-data status, judgment ceiling, and action ceiling. H1/H4/H5 may enter the specified routes only through the corresponding H-instance in each case. G1–G4 can support empirical claims only through a G-instance frozen before results. An interface card can raise a question. Completing its fields cannot establish facts about the object.

## 13.3 The Seven Gates as the Only Main Workflow

Run the seven gates for each specific proposition in dependency order. After one gate fails, dependent later gates may only be marked `not_run`, and the corresponding judgments and requirements for action stop or are downgraded. A later `not_run` cannot raise the ceiling lowered by the failed gate. Acknowledging that the object or evidence failed a gate while allowing dependent later procedures to report their own pass is also prohibited. Judgments that do not depend on the failed step must retain independent evidence and undergo review of their intended use. Another proposition passing cannot conceal this proposition’s failure. If overall source status, a shared factual premise, the object boundary, or lawful conditions for use of the task cease to hold, all dependent outputs stop together. When the object, evidence version, affected positions, or intended use changes substantially, rerun the workflow from the earliest gate affected.

| ID | Gate | Core Question | Output Ceiling If It Does Not Pass |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IG1 | Effective Object Gate | Do the object, boundary, nine-axis scale, time window, and K support the current judgment? | Redefine the object, leave the scale unknown, or review object conversion |
| IG2 | Evidence Traceability Gate | Are sources, materials, observations, evidence, counterexamples, cases, and judgments distinct and traceable? | Organization of materials, candidate questions, or hypotheses to test |
| IG3 | Affected Positions Gate | Which positions will see their circumstances change through direct, indirect, second-order, cross-domain, or intertemporal effects? | Supplement the accounting of impacts. Suspend strong judgments and high-impact uses |
| IG4 | Power and Anti-Retaliation Gate | Can people in low-power positions safely add evidence, rebut claims, refuse, stop, and appeal? | Draw no adverse inference from silence. Lower the levels of publication and judgment |
| IG5 | Mediation and Reflexivity Gate | Do metrics, platforms, AI, public evaluation, or the act of diagnosis change the evidence and the object? | High reflexivity remains unresolved. Restate the judgment as a conditional state transition |
| IG6 | Strong-Judgment Procedure Gate | Have all SJ1–SJ8 items been submitted for the proposition whose intended use and foreseeable impacts trigger the strong-judgment procedure? Public dissemination requires closer scrutiny. It does not by itself elevate all analyses to strong judgments | Stop the relevant strong judgments and high-impact uses. Review separately any comparative judgment that stands independently and meets the requirements for its use |
| IG7 | Action Ceiling Gate | What is the most that may be required? Which actions are prohibited, and when are stopping, appeal, and rollback called for? | Description, gaps in evidence, no action, or continued deliberation |

Passing all seven gates indicates only that the applicable recording requirements have no gaps. It does not establish that the conclusion is true. Empirical propositions are still judged under their proposition contracts and instance evidence. Normative objectives still require explicit N premises. Real-world action still requires choice, C12, J, and O procedures. In particular, IG7 can only record whether the external normative and authorization layers meet their requirements. The tool workflow cannot mark an action as “authorized” on its own.

IG6 has a specific applicability rule. An ordinary L1/L2 output may mark it `not_applicable`, but the record must state why the strong-judgment procedure is not triggered in this case. This does not block IG7. Once IG6 is marked `passed`, the same run record must link a complete SJ1–SJ8, with every item marked `passed`. A “checked” string or an empty rationale cannot substitute for the eight results.

The scope of identification for IG3 is determined under §14.4.1. Positions that section requires to be listed in full must be listed in full; for other positions, the record must state how far they were traced and why tracing stopped. For explanation and diagnosis tasks without a comparison of options, the test is whether further tracing could change the conclusion or its grade, the scope of use, or protective arrangements.

## 13.4 The Five Gates and 13 Steps for Elaborating a Diagnosis

Run the five gates and 13 steps when the object is complex, responsibility is high, reflexivity is high, or the seven gates call for more detailed evidence. The five gates are local checks within this elaboration. They do not generate a second set of main workflow statuses.

| ID | Elaboration Gate | Material That Must Be Developed | Corresponding Main Gates |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FG1 | Object Gate | Object, boundary, scale, and time window | IG1 |
| FG2 | Evidence Gate | Cost of signals, sources, weights, and review | IG2 |
| FG3 | Scale Gate | Nine-axis scale, objects and evidence at the scale, and changes in scale | IG1, IG2 |
| FG4 | Responsibility Gate | Effects on rights/resources, contrary conditions, repair windows, appeals, and review | IG3, IG4, IG6 |
| FG5 | Observation Gate | Baseline before observation, reactions during observation, reactions after publication, and a ceiling on recursion | IG5 |

The 13 steps form an auditable sequence from the real-world question to write-back.

| ID | Step | Minimum Deliverable |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DS01 | Start With the Question | A real-world question in language outside the framework, the requester, and the intended use |
| DS02 | Identify the Object and Boundary | Effective object, subsystems, higher-level environment, and external channels |
| DS03 | Separate Signal Types | Separate columns for facts, behavior, resources, feedback, narratives, feelings, conjecture, and noise |
| DS04 | Record Participation in Observation | Awareness of being observed, observer power, impacts of publication, and candidate responses |
| DS05 | Identify the Dominant Constraint | Candidate constraints, the selected constraint, evidence for selection, and competing constraints |
| DS06 | Record the State and Window | References to S0–S6 prototypes for applicable objects, positions within the state, parallel subsystems, and contrary conditions. If inapplicable, state why and retain the domain’s own states and windows |
| DS07 | Examine the Core Structure | Anchor, uptake layer, transmission chain, boundary, and feedback write-back |
| DS08 | Identify Key Variables for Protection | Variables to protect, paths to harm, resources, redundancy, and paths for repair/return flows |
| DS09 | Record Uptake and Repayment | Cost bearers, recipients of return flows, rights to stop, long-term overextension, and shifting of harms |
| DS10 | Identify Hidden Risks and Boundary Types | Maintenance debt, mismatches between fast and slow processes, metric substitution, distorted summaries, and candidate object conversions |
| DS11 | Propose Candidate Mechanisms | Competing mechanisms that the material permits and that could change the judgment, the evidence each requires, evidence that weakens them, and rules for eliminating them. If no plausible competing explanation exists, state the scope of the search and the failure conditions. Do not invent mechanisms to meet a quota |
| DS12 | Anticipate Branches | Triggers, early signals, contrary conditions, repair windows, and minimum requirements for action |
| DS13 | Produce the Output and Write Back | Output in ordinary language, triggers for revision, scope of the record, review, withdrawal, and rollback |

When DS06 uses S0–S6, it must do so only for human intentional collectives to which the prototypes apply. The prototypes must not be forced onto other objects. S0–S6 must not be treated as levels of maturity or as fate. Identifying an important variable for protection in DS08 can generate only a requirement for protection. Identifying uptake or overextension in DS09 cannot generate a duty to make sacrifices or establish full responsibility. Candidate mechanisms in DS11 must not bypass the causal contract. Branches in DS12 do not describe a certain future. If DS13 lacks review and withdrawal procedures, the judgment must not be entered into organizational records, eligibility, allocation, or public memory.

## 13.5 The Strong-Judgment Kit

Strong judgments are defined by their uses and foreseeable impacts. Tone, length, and confidence of expression do not determine the category. A judgment enters the strong-judgment procedure if it is intended or reasonably foreseeable for use in real-world action or decisions about eligibility, rights, or resource allocation. The same applies if it has a material impact on a specific subject’s reputation and place in public memory, or if it is embedded in automated, organizational, or institutional decisions. Check the actual use of intermediate scores, recommendations, or material screening when they affect these decisions downstream. Public dissemination calls for greater scrutiny. Assess it in light of the object evaluated, factual allegations, identifiability, reach, and consequences. Publication alone is insufficient to raise every analytical output to a strong judgment. Ordinary work can reach definite conclusions when evaluating arguments, explaining mechanisms, or articulating normative views, provided the uses and impacts above are absent and the evidence and review meet the task’s requirements. Calling an output commentary, research, or a draft does not exempt it if it actually meets the strong-judgment conditions. A strong-judgment candidate may be registered only at L3, with all seven gates passed and each of the following eight items passed individually. It still confers no authorization to act in reality.

| ID | Component | Required Question |
| --- | --- | --- |
| SJ1 | Contrary conditions | What circumstances would weaken, reverse, or lead to withdrawal of the judgment? |
| SJ2 | Repair window | Where is the window, what supports it, and when does it cease to hold? |
| SJ3 | Evidence requirements | What evidence supports the judgment, what evidence is missing, and what evidence weighs against it? |
| SJ4 | Access to appeal | Who can file an appeal, who is responsible, what is the time limit, and can the outcome change? |
| SJ5 | Anti-retaliation protection | Which activities are protected, how is protection monitored, and how can retaliation be reported independently and remedied? |
| SJ6 | Right to add evidence | How can evidence be supplemented, versions corrected, and downstream records synchronized? |
| SJ7 | Trigger for external review | When is review triggered, who reviews independently, and can they access the materials and stop the result from taking effect? |
| SJ8 | Rollback and write-back | Which state is restored, who carries out the repair, which version is authoritative, and how are updates synchronized? |

If any item is missing, IG6 fails for that strong judgment. The judgment must not enter the category of strong-judgment candidate or retain its original high-impact use. It may be recorded only as an internal hypothesis, an open assertion, or a candidate mechanism within the limits of its materials and actual use. An L2 comparative judgment may remain if it does not depend on the missing item, has independent grounds, and does not trigger a strong-judgment use. Failure of a common premise still blocks all dependent judgments. Renaming or splitting must not be used to evade that limit. Access to appeal does not count as an effective appeal if it cannot change a record or action. Being external in name does not establish independence. A statement, apology, or release of a new version does not amount to actual rollback and repair.

## 13.6 Diagnostic Levels

| ID | Level | When It Applies | Output Ceiling |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | Lightweight description and exploratory questions | Low complexity, low impact, reversibility, and limited information | Description, questions, and the next observation |
| L2 | Standard reviewable judgment | A structural comparison is needed, but the judgment will not be used for action carrying high responsibility | A comparative judgment within stated limits or a candidate mechanism |
| L3 | Strong-judgment candidate carrying high responsibility | Judgments with substantively high impact or embedded in automated, organizational, or institutional decisions. Public dissemination is reviewed in light of the object, factual allegations, identifiability, and consequences | A strong-judgment candidate after the seven gates and the kit pass |

A higher level adds responsibility for recording and does not make a judgment “closer to the truth.” The level must be downgraded if evidence coverage declines, the object undergoes conversion, reflexivity becomes uncontrolled, or appeals or anti-retaliation protection fail. Urgency does not permit omitting the object, protection, or stopping conditions. When waiting is costly, the tool may only propose the narrowest, withdrawable, monitorable requirement for action, separately from the applicable authorization.

## 13.7 Six Formal Tools

| ID | Tool | Purpose and Limits |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `TOOL-EVIDENCE-LEDGER` | Source–Evidence Ledger | Keep sources, materials, observations, evidence, counterexamples, coverage, and uncertainty separate. It does not prove a proposition |
| `TOOL-OPEN-ASSERTION` | Open Assertion Record | Retain a provisional judgment that can be questioned, falsified, or withdrawn. Do not use it for action carrying high responsibility |
| `TOOL-CLAIM-VALIDATION` | Proposition Validation Form | Register proposition type, evidence, counterevidence, falsification conditions, impacts, and ceilings separately |
| `TOOL-FORECAST-REGISTRY` | Forecast Registration | Freeze the prediction, window, triggers, early signals, contrary signals, and targets for revision |
| `TOOL-STRESS-TEST` | Counterexample and Scale Stress Test | Use boundary cases, adversarial explanations, and changes in scale to locate failures and narrow the scope |
| `TOOL-AI-BOUNDARY` | AI Output Boundaries | Require disclosure of sources, identification of missing material and the responsible human, and recording of uncertainty, prohibited uses, review, and withdrawal |

The evidence ledger must not turn “has a source” into “is supported.” An open assertion cannot substitute for proposition validation. A failed prediction must be written back to the relevant proposition. An internal stress test is only a wind tunnel and does not supply external empirical support. Tools may cite one another’s record IDs, but must not validate one another circularly.

Automatic validation is suited to checking closed fields, whether references resolve, consistency of versions and statuses, and whether calculations can be reproduced. It cannot replace assessment of materials, testimony from people affected, causal identification, or choices about values. Programs should do the work that can be done mechanically so that readers can examine the actual grounds for judgments. Complete logs and clear prose can be kept separately with references between them.

## 13.8 Limits on the Use of AI

AI can organize materials, check fields, compare versions, generate candidate questions, list alternative explanations, and find inconsistencies in formatting. Where the evidence and normative premises allow, it should also compare mechanisms, form refutable judgments, recommend options, and draft complete institutional arrangements, plans, and commentary. AI must not invent sources, treat information recalled from training as current evidence, express consent on someone’s behalf, interpret silence as consent, take the place of a deciding subject or authorizing authority, or hide uncertainty. It must not make high-risk real-world decisions independently.

AI outputs must state what materials were used, what is missing, which content is observation/inference/analogy, who is responsible for the final judgment, how it may be used, and when it is to be reviewed or withdrawn. Where disclosure of identity, retaliation risk, or justified opacity is involved, protective omissions must remain distinguishable from “no evidence.”

Responsibility for AI use should also reflect the actual division of work among those who provide materials, decide objectives and permissions, maintain systems, carry out actions, and review results. Facilities and procedures that perform technical steps do not become subjects capable of bearing moral or legal responsibility merely because they output recommendations or execute instructions. Organizations legally capable of bearing a specified responsibility must be assessed separately according to their institutional status and the matter at issue. Greater automation does not put all responsibility on the person who clicks last. Reviewers must have sufficient materials, time, capability, and authority to stop actions in practice. The organization must also provide the resources needed for these conditions.

## 13.9 Run Records and Failure Semantics

Each tool run creates a `flow_execution_record` with a closed set of fields. It registers object and scale records, the L1–L3 level, IT1–IT4 results, IG1–IG7 results, whether the five gates and 13 steps were run, whether SJ1–SJ8 were triggered, evidence, alternative explanations, uncertainty, output categories, judgment and action ceilings, normative handoff, appeal, rollback, and status.

The action ceiling uses a closed lattice of output ceilings: `description_only`, `diagnostic_only`, and `requirements_only`. Each level has fixed permitted output categories and has `can_authorize=false` and `can_execute=false`. The caller must not claim “already authorized” in free text or expand the permitted categories on its own. `action_requirements` only specifies conditions for the external choice and authorization procedures. It is not an executable command.

A normative handoff must link to verifiable external choice, C12, and authorization records, or state explicitly that these are missing. References must specify the object, action, scope, time, and actual grounds for verification. Vague labels, self-reports of having passed, or circular references to the same record cannot establish permission. The implementation determines the reference format. The tool is still prohibited from issuing its own authorization.

Outcome statuses must remain distinct. `passed` means the recording requirements at that gate were met. `failed` means the step ran and failed. `not_run` means it was not run because of an earlier gate or a scope restriction. `unknown`, `not_applicable`, `not_observable`, and `withheld_for_protection` remain four distinct missing-data statuses. Failure must not be rewritten as inapplicability. Concealment for protection must not be rewritten as absence.

The run record’s `authorization_effect` is always `none`. Even when external references for normative choice, C12, J, and O exist, this record only checks them and hands them off. It does not issue authorization. All reviewed outputs must retain an accessible appeal and a means of actual withdrawal or correction. A tool conclusion that cannot be withdrawn must not be put to a high-impact use that is difficult to reverse.

## 13.10 The Nine Gates for Multi-Circle Projection

The seven gates are the only main workflow for explanation and diagnosis. When a task explicitly calls for dynamic projection, conditional foresight, or bounded choice, elaborate that workflow with the nine gates. The nine gates must not bypass the seven gates’ requirements for the object, evidence, affected positions, power, reflexivity, the strong-judgment procedure, and the action ceiling.

| Gate | Core Check | Output on Failure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DF1 Freeze the Facts | Event type, source, time, evidence cutoff, and disputes | List of factual questions |
| DF2 Joint Object | Actors, circles, relations, membership, and K | Candidate groupings or a return to a single focus |
| DF3 Two Channels and Clocks | Material and experience–meaning conditions relevant to the task, clocks, and unknowns. Mark irrelevant items inapplicable with a reason | Retain the state snapshot. Suspend propagation that depends on missing conditions and review independent paths separately |
| DF4 Propagation Through Mechanisms | Channels, thresholds, delay, feedback, and cascades | Candidate mechanisms and observations that can distinguish them |
| DF5 Branching Paths | Conditions, parent and child nodes, and early and contrary signals | Parallel paths without forced ordering |
| DF6 Forecast Registration | Target, horizon, simple baseline, calibration, and write-back | Scenario projection only |
| DF7 Normative Choice | N, PF, affected people, options, and no action | Suspend recommendations blocked by the gap. Retain evaluations that stand independently, conditional recommendations under explicit premises, and requirements for more evidence. This confers no permission to execute |
| DF8 Authorization and Execution | J, O, responsibility, stopping, rollback, and remedy | External handoff without execution |
| DF9 Outcome Write-Back | Results, comparison with the baseline, deviations, downgrades, and retirement | Retain the failure and open a new version |

The tool can propose candidate circles at DF2 and variable candidates at DF5. Every candidate retains the status `candidate`. If DF6 does not pass, outputs must not use forecast language such as “will happen” or “expected to prove accurate.” If DF7 or DF8 does not pass, outputs must not give executable instructions. DF9 does not permit overwriting the original run.

## 13.11 Limits on AI in Dynamic Runs

AI can organize materials, check original passages that can be located, find gaps in fields, generate and compare competing paths, check contract consistency, and calculate registered metrics. Under explicit normative premises it can also form well-grounded local judgments and recommendations and draft complete texts. AI cannot certify a real-world event solely by its own generation, self-report, or internal procedure. It cannot diagnose personality, disclose information on behalf of those concerned, impose circle boundaries on others through its own classifications, treat simulations as facts, settle disputed value premises for others, or issue authorization. Whether external material supports a particular fact must be judged separately according to the material’s status, method, counterexamples, and intended use. Faithfulness to a source does not automatically establish that what it says about the world is true. For a variable automatically proposed by AI, the system must record its status as a model candidate, the minimal test, and prohibited uses. It must not be upgraded before the corresponding external evidence is available.

### 13.11.1 Checking AI Materials and the Chain of Tool Actions

When checking a generated answer, examine separately whether the source exists, whether the citation details are correct, whether the original passage supports the sentence, and whether the fact asserted in the sentence has adequate grounds. A source’s silence on the matter does not establish that the sentence is wrong. Accurately restating a mistaken source does not establish the facts. Where local knowledge, oral accounts, or protected materials have no public webpage, retain appropriate means of verification and limits on public disclosure. They must not be treated as nonexistent on that basis alone.

Retrieval augmentation can improve access to materials and performance on a task. Consistency or uncertainty checks can also detect certain errors. Their effectiveness remains limited by retrieval coverage, shared sources, and the types of error. An error repeated reliably can appear consistent. Several models repeating the same inaccurate material do not supply several independent pieces of evidence. A formal checker can confirm a derivation under stated premises, but cannot establish that those premises hold in reality.

For automated action, record the recommendation, tool request, application execution, external state, and consequences separately. The model writing “stopped” does not establish that the executor received a stop instruction. An interface showing “deleted” does not establish that downstream copies are gone. A person changing a recommendation does not establish that the change took effect before an irreversible action. Permissions for actual protection and correction must be exercisable in the corresponding system.

### 13.11.2 Task Endpoints and a Verifiable Execution Chain

The endpoint for verifying a tool chain is determined by the user’s request or the institutional task. If the request is only for a draft, delivery can be complete when the draft has been saved correctly, can be opened, and its content meets the requirements. If the task requires changing an external record, success returned by the tool must still be confirmed by the state of the corresponding external object. If the task requires actual remedy, verify that the resources, rights, or services reach those concerned. Actual completion of a narrower endpoint need not wait for every consequence in people’s lives. A success message in the interface cannot substitute for completion of a broader endpoint.

| Stage | Minimum Object of Verification | Typical Break |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Form the recommendation | Objective, materials, object to be changed, and permitted scope | Treating generated text as already adopted |
| Make the tool request | Specific action, parameters, current object version, and permissions | Sending a correct recommendation to the wrong object |
| Execute in the application | Whether the request was received, executed, or rejected, and how repeated requests are handled | A request arrives but is queued, fails, or is executed repeatedly |
| Check external state | Whether the target and dependent objects changed as intended | A local marker changes while actual execution still reads the old state |
| Check the task outcome | The specified benefit or protection endpoint and remaining problems | A corrected record takes effect, but the missed opportunity is not restored |

For concurrent operations across tools, first distinguish operations that can read independently, operations that can modify independently, and operations that write to the same object. Changes to shared state must preserve ordering, version prerequisites, or conflict handling. Before an irreversible action, verify again that permissions are still valid and check the stopping conditions. A request timing out means its outcome is unknown. Do not assume it was not executed and repeat it. Check the request identifier or object state first, then decide whether to retry. These are responsibilities for execution. They do not require every application to use the same log format.

Stopping has its own completion conditions. Record separately the issuing of a stop instruction, acknowledgment by the executor, termination of actions already in progress, and remedy of consequences that have already occurred. If only the first two steps are confirmed, report that scope accurately. If winding down safely requires a limited action under a separate responsibility, continue it on its own grounds. Stopping the main task must not mean abandoning the necessary continuation. When repair fails, keep a record of the object for which repair failed and of the real-world consequences. A new, normal output must not overwrite them.

[^tn-diagnosis-14]: Translator's note: Diagnosis here denotes a nonclinical assessment within the framework.

[^tn-contract-14]: Translator's note: A contract here is an analytical agreement registered and frozen before results, akin to pre-registration.

<a id="part_14"></a>

# Part 14 Normative Choice

The preceding structural grammar, empirical question families, scale transformations, mechanisms of operation, and human variables address “how objects exist, operate, and change, and what grounds we have for explaining them in these ways.” This Part asks a different question. Among several possible directions, who may choose, on what grounds, whom does the choice bind, and how do we stop and repair if it fails?

The two kinds of question must be kept separate. An explanation can be highly reliable and still be insufficient to confer authority to act. A goal can receive wide support and still breach rights floors, shift costs to low-power positions, or exceed the decision maker’s jurisdiction. Normative choice has its own contract[^tn-contract-15] between explanation and real-world action. It cannot be treated as an appendix to diagnosis.

The requirements in this Part belong to three levels with different scopes. Principles of argument state what a normative judgment must distinguish: facts and what ought to be, the value premises used and their conflicts, and beneficiaries and those who bear the costs; where options are chosen, they also include the options, no action among them. Analytical recommendations for deliberation are bound by them as well. Substantive safeguards state what real-world adoption must actually have: valid authorization covering the action, and the substance of the protections referred to by PF-1–PF-10, including identification of affected and low-power positions, safe submission and anti-retaliation, genuine refusal and exit, accessible appeal and independent review, adding evidence and correcting records, rollback and remedy, and action ceilings and expiry. The record format states how this framework registers the above for its own `authorized` status, for example by pointing to the no-action option through a unique `no_action_option_id` and having exactly ten `protection_floor_bindings`. Other implementations that carry equivalent responsibilities for protection, tracing, and review may adopt different formats. Whether they are equivalent must be confirmed by independent review, not self-reported by the implementer. If deleting a requirement would change who is protected, who bears the costs, who has the right to decide, who can stop, or who can correct errors, it does not belong to the record format.

## 14.1 Facts Do Not Entail What Ought to Be Done

Factual propositions describe “what is.” Normative premises state “what is worth pursuing, protecting, or prohibiting.” Claims that “a structure is stable,” “an option is more efficient,” “the system retains a certain type,” or “a risk is rising” cannot directly entail “therefore this should be done.” Facts have a defined role in choice. They determine whether an option is possible, what its consequences might be, and the extent of risk and uncertainty. They do not choose values or create authorization on their own.

Any process that moves from explanation or diagnosis to real-world action must therefore go through four conversions. O1 freezes the facts, evidence, scale, and uncertainty to be used. O2 makes explicit the N1–N5 premises adopted at runtime, conflicts of value, beneficiaries, and those who bear the costs. O3 compares options including no action and reviews the J axis, authorization, jurisdiction, validity period, and conflicts of interest. A separate normative choice record then freezes the decision. Only then can O4 turn the approved option into bounded execution that is reversible, monitorable, stoppable, subject to appeal and rollback, and ends on expiry.

The severity of a diagnosis provides no direct route to action. High model confidence provides no route to broader permission. Stronger evidence can raise the ceiling for descriptive judgments, but cannot raise the action ceiling by itself.

C12 checks the validity of the normative bridge. C12 does not choose values. It checks only whether the descriptive conclusion, current evidence, explicit normative premises, conflicts of value, the protection floor, the J axis, options, no action, O1–O4, the action ceiling, and conditions for correction all meet their requirements at the same time. Least harm and proportionality must be explicitly selected, explained, and reviewed in the specific choice record. They must not be left implicit in C12.

## 14.2 Three Distinct Processes of Selection and Choice

Selection and choice cover at least three structurally distinct processes. Conflating them personifies natural or distributed processes, expands personal preferences into public power, or reduces public procedures to the will of those with power.

`SEL-SYS` has no unified deciding subject. It refers to variation or differences producing different outcomes through environments, feedback, competition, replication, and retention mechanisms. Examples include natural selection, differential retention in markets, and content spreading as a result of algorithmic rewards. The unit of selection, environment, retention mechanism, time window, and stochastic null model must be registered. One may say that a form is retained, replicated, or eliminated. One must not say that the system “considers it better.” Retention by itself does not establish normative justification, nor do survival, stability, popularity, or efficiency. The action ceiling for `SEL-SYS` is a description of the selection process. It cannot enter `authorized` or include an `external_action` option.

`SEL-AGT` is decision-making by a subject under constraints. The deciding subject must be identifiable and can decide only within their own permissions, genuine options, territory, and validity period. An apparent “choice” must not be recorded as free consent if it arises from coercion, lock-in through defaults, deprivation of information, insufficient capability, unsafe refusal, or blocked exit. Failure to exit does not amount to consent. Entering a process does not amount to continuing consent. One subject’s choice does not automatically bind others.

`SEL-GOV` is collective governance authorized through a procedure. It addresses common affairs. The fact that they are common does not establish that the majority can represent everyone or that the scope of impact equals the scope of jurisdiction. A governance decision must make it possible to trace its boundaries, how the agenda was formed, who has authority to represent, which positions can participate, how dissent is preserved, how minorities are protected, how conflicts of interest are handled, who executes and reviews it, and when authorization expires. Numbers, loudness, efficiency, claims to represent, and legal form cannot by themselves substitute for bounded public authorization.

The three types have strict boundaries. `SEL-SYS` cannot acquire intent or normative justification by borrowing the language of subjects. `SEL-AGT` cannot decide for others beyond the subject’s own permissions. `SEL-GOV` cannot bypass procedure and present governance as an enlarged version of personal choice.

## 14.3 Registering N1–N5 Premises at Runtime

N1–N5 do not operate automatically as background assumptions. Each choice record must list in `public_value_premises` the IDs actually adopted for this choice and say whether each is an objective, a constraint, or a veto gate. A normative premise that was not registered must not appear silently in the conclusion.

N1, “Explanation Does Not Confer Authority to Act,” vetoes any direct move from facts to action. On its own, N1 can yield only no action, a need for more evidence, or continued deliberation. It cannot generate an affirmative proposal.

N2, “Protection Must Not Be Downgraded,” requires scale elevation and action to maintain or strengthen existing protections at low-power positions. If the infrastructure for protection is missing, narrow the action and preserve the protection.

N3, “High-Impact Judgments Must Be Correctable,” requires genuine access to appeal, independent review, stopping, withdrawal, rollback, remedy, and version write-back.

N4, “Love and Undertaking Must Not Be Conscripted,” prohibits invoking love, responsibility, care, or the persistence of a system to demand unilateral, unlimited sacrifice without an option to exit.

Bounded responsibility under N4 precludes demands made in the name of love, care, or system persistence for unilateral, unlimited sacrifice without an option to exit. It does not cancel bounded commitments, obligations of support, contractual duties, or remedial responsibilities that have independent grounds. Ending or transferring a responsibility should specify who will actually take over for dependents, for how long, and with what remaining risks. This requirement must not be used to force those harmed to remain exposed to harm. Capability, causal contribution, benefit, fault, and duties of remedy should be justified separately. 〔Studies 06.06, 09.02, 10.07, 21.06, 23.01–23.07〕

N5, “Persistence and Systemic Selection Do Not Establish Normative Justification,” limits stability, efficiency, coherence, and outcomes of systemic selection to evidence about consequences. They must not substitute for a judgment of normative justification.

Premises drawn from N can conflict. For example, quickly limiting losses can conflict with full participation. Expanding public benefits can entail risks to the rights of those in minority positions.
