At k=0 there is a root at 1, so the system is not asymptotically stable. For k<0, p(1,k)<0, while p(λ,k) is positive for sufficiently large positive λ. There is therefore a real root greater than 1. For sufficiently small positive k, the root at 1 enters the unit circle as λ=1−k+O(k²). The remaining roots, originally at 0, stay inside the circle. Small positive gains are therefore stable. When d=0, the only root 1−k directly gives 0<k<2.

For positive k, write a boundary root as λ=e^{iθ}, with 0<θ<2π. From k=λ^d(1−λ) we obtain

k=2sin(θ/2)exp{i[(d+1/2)θ−π/2]}.

For k to be positive and real, θ=(π+4πm)/(2d+1) with m=0,…,d. The first boundary at positive gain comes from the smallest angle and its conjugate angle, giving k\_c(d). The roots vary continuously with the parameter and are bounded at finite parameter values. They must pass through the boundary before leaving the unit circle.

We must also rule out renewed stability at higher gains. On the unit circle, p\_λ=λ^{d−1}[(d+1)λ−d] is nonzero, so boundary roots are simple. Implicit differentiation gives

d log|λ|/dk = (2d+1)(1−cosθ)/{k|(d+1)e^{iθ}−d|²}>0.

Thus all crossings at positive k go outward, with no re-entry. Together with stability at small gains, this gives the necessary and sufficient interval stated above. Equality at the boundary leaves a nondecaying mode. A root modulus slightly below 1 in floating-point arithmetic is no basis for including the boundary. Above the boundary a growing mode exists, while the special all-zero history still remains zero.

With k=0.8 fixed, the critical values for d=0 and 1 are 2 and 1, respectively, and the system is stable. The critical values for d=2 and 3 are approximately 0.618034 and 0.445042, respectively, and the system is unstable. For an all-ones initial history, the first step in all four cases gives 0.2. The feedback term already affects the state in the model, while convergence differs. Even when the system is stable, the result still places no bound on transient peaks or costs and does not establish acceptability to people or real-world repair. Time-varying delays, stochastic disturbances, nonlinearity, and constraints on execution require new analysis.

## C.11 Historical Increments and Omissions in the Current Representation

Use the same 961 equiprobable initial states, but let each queue serve at most 5 tasks per round with no new arrivals. Advance the full state by F(q₁,q₂)=((q₁−5)\_+,(q₂−5)\_+). The prediction target is the mean after the second round. Compare four inputs after the first round: the current mean, the current mean with the initial mean, the full current state, and the full current state with the initial mean. Under squared loss, outputting the conditional mean of the target for each input fiber attains the minimum expected loss.

| Prediction Input | Minimum Mean Squared Error Under the Given Distribution |
| --- | ---: |
| Current mean | 0.579016561 |
| Current mean and initial mean | 0.271143397 |
| Full current state of both queues | 0 |
| Full current state of both queues and initial mean | 0 |

The calculation advances each of the 961 initial states and retains its original weight. It does not make the states after the first round uniform again. In particular, (0,20) advances to (0,15) and has a next mean of 5. The state (10,15) advances to (5,10) and has a next mean of 2.5. Both current means are 7.5, while the initial means differ. The historical mean helps distinguish differences collapsed by the current projection. The full current state already suffices to determine the future.

This establishes that a historical predictive increment can arise from an omission in the current representation. It does not establish that real systems universally lack memory. Real data still require attention to measurement, confounding, temporal order, leakage, and model selection. If the full current carrier is unavailable, one cannot claim to have ruled out a historical increment. If earlier experience affects the outcome through a current skill and the residual disappears once that skill is represented sufficiently, the result limits only the additional prediction given the skill. It does not negate the total effect of earlier experience or rule out learning in general.

## C.12 Three Limits on Composing Local Successes

Whether conversions can compose to give the same final answer, whether causal effects can link into a chain, and whether actions can be jointly executed with available resources concern functions, joint responses, and sets of constraints, respectively. They can occur in the same workflow. A generic status of “connected” cannot make them interchangeable.

See §2.4.2 for the causal counterexample. The types s∈{0,1} have equal weight, with m(a)=a(1−s) and y(a,m)=sm. The average effect from treatment to mediator and the average effect from mediator to outcome are both 1/2, whereas the effect of the full chain is 0 because different types carry the two effects. This rules out multiplying edge means unconditionally. It does not withdraw an independently identified total effect.

The resource counterexample consists of two unit tasks, each taking a full period. Both must be completed before the end of the first period, which has capacity of only 1. Each task is feasible alone, but there is no feasible joint schedule. Joint execution becomes feasible if capacity in that period rises to 2, or if one deadline is legitimately moved to the second period and that period has capacity of 1. If both original deadlines remain, adding capacity in the second period cannot make up for the original deadline. The conclusion follows directly from the stated constraints. It does not certify real-world demand, capacity, or authorization.

**F3 Common feasible action proposition.** For a finite, single-round deterministic task, let X\_y be a nonempty observation class and A\_ok(x) the set of eligible actions for each state. There exists an action depending only on y that is eligible for every x∈X\_y if and only if ∩\_{x∈X\_y}A\_ok(x) is nonempty.

Proof. If such an action a exists, it belongs to the eligible set of every compatible state and therefore to the common intersection. Conversely, choose any a in the nonempty intersection. It is eligible in every compatible state, and choosing it does not require knowing which state actually holds. Nonemptiness of each state’s set separately is only a necessary condition. For example, if the eligible sets of two states are {左} and {右}, meaning left and right, respectively, each has an option but they share no action. If both allow an authorized preservation action, the intersection can be nonempty while the state remains incompletely identified.

The eligible sets in the proposition must already incorporate the resources, protections, and valid permissions required by the task. Writing an action in mathematical terms cannot substitute for these real-world conditions. The proposition concerns only the listed compatible states and cannot guarantee that unknown states will never occur. When undertaken lawfully, further observation, waiting, or preservation can change the information and policy sets. None of these occurs automatically. Probability-based choices that tolerate risk, randomization, and sequential policies are analyzed separately according to their own objectives. Representational sufficiency, computability, accessibility, implementability, resources, and authority to adopt must be justified separately for the specific use.

<a id="part_20"></a>

# Appendix D A Map of Domain Explanations and Applications

This map draws on existing research across 32 domains. Each section preserves the questions of its domain before connecting them to the applicable limits on representation, evidence, normative judgment, and action in the main text. The domains do not bear responsibility for validating one another. Several studies citing the same original work, data, or case still form one linked chain of evidence.

## D.01 The Framework Itself, Explanatory Tasks, and Borrowing From Theory

In the main text, see §1.3–1.10, §2.2–2.7, and §16.15–16.16. Research entries cover 01.01–01.08.

Start by asking which outcome needs explaining. Do not begin with whichever vocabulary is most familiar. Why equipment failed, why a decision took shape, who bears the consequences, and how the people concerned understand what they experienced are related but distinct tasks. Technical mechanisms, institutional authority, and lived experience can correct one another or each provide the main account of a particular part of the problem. Structure, interests, power, psychology, culture, and technology should not have permanent priority before evidence is examined.

Borrowing from theory requires distinguishing at least guidance for observation, definitions, testable mechanisms, formal inferences, and normative choices. The same term appearing in two theories does not guarantee that the objects, variables, or permitted inferences are the same. A formal mapping should state which relation it preserves, which distinctions it loses, and what circumstances would change the conclusion. If a domain method already answers the question sufficiently, the framework can limit its role to checking sources and boundaries. It need not introduce additional circles into the analysis.

In a teaching example, an automatic reminder reduces missed tasks, but appeals still cannot change errors. Improvement on the technical task can coexist with inadequate procedural remedy. To ask whether users are treated with respect, one also needs evidence of their actual circumstances and what they say. Calling all three outcomes “a better system” would obscure the differences. It would likewise be a mistake to let a shortcoming in one invalidate all the gains.

Keep historical adoption of a theory, mention in a catalog, compatibility discovered later, and new connections proposed by an analyst separate. Versions in different texts do not have the same identity merely because they share a number. Obtaining one manuscript cannot supply wording in another source that has yet to be checked. Successful procedural checks, complete files, and a closed numbering scheme establish only the corresponding facts about the engineering work. They do not establish that the meanings are correct or that use in reality improves outcomes.

An analysis can yield answers at the conceptual, textual, formal, domain-empirical, and practical levels in succession. The first four answers, where established, do not disappear because the last has yet to be obtained. Clearer writing or more material cannot stand in for practical benefits. To judge the value of an added method, fix the actual material processed, the task, and comparable resources. Retain a strong domain method as a comparison and account for undelivered work, misjudgments, waiting, and maintenance burden.

Research topics. 01.01 Original questions and changes in objectives. 01.02 The status of concepts, metaphors, mechanisms, and theorems. 01.03 Deliberate exclusions and gaps in the historical record. 01.04 Explanatory priorities and theoretical colonization. 01.05 The framework itself, its application layer, and its tool layer. 01.06 Boundaries, identity, and construction of circles. 01.07 Counterexamples, failure, replacement, and exit. 01.08 Conceptual frameworks and the engineering of Skill packages.

## D.02 Knowledge, Concepts, Context, and Epistemic Position

In the main text, see §2.1–2.7, §4.8, and §16.6 and 16.15. Research entries cover 02.01–02.08.

Experience, reasoning, practice, embodied experience, interpretation of texts, and normative argument carry distinct burdens of proof. A formal derivation must disclose its premises and rules. An empirical judgment must show how observations support the claim. Interpretive research must consider the text, context, and competing readings together. Normative argument must state the reasons and positions affected. These forms of inquiry can constrain one another. They cannot all be compressed into one measure of predictive performance.

Falsification, paradigms, and research programs offer criticism at different scales. A local counterexample may refute an auxiliary hypothesis or require changing the object or measurement. Whether it undermines a broader theory depends on the original claim’s scope and dependencies. A different question can yield valuable new research, but its results cannot be recorded retroactively as success in the old test. Usefulness, actionability, consensus, and truth are distinct standards.

Language describes and can also constitute action. Making a promise, naming something, granting eligibility, and reporting a fact require different positions from which to speak, rules, and evidence. Someone saying “I consent” may be expressing their wishes sincerely, yet under particular pressures this may be insufficient for valid authorization. Understanding what they mean cannot substitute for checking the institutional conditions. A mismatch in language also cannot serve directly to diagnose personality or intelligence.

Epistemic position affects who can access material, what questions can be asked, and whose statements are more readily believed. Better conditions for hearing people, translating, and speaking safely can increase available knowledge. Statements by people who are excluded should still be assessed claim by claim. Exclusion neither disqualifies a statement in advance nor grants it an automatic claim to truth. Non-disclosure for protection can coexist with credible verification.

Methods can correct one another while retaining genuinely different theoretical commitments. The influence of position on knowledge production does not imply that objects can be made up at will. Reasons can enter a causal explanation without removing the separate task of understanding meaning. A reliable record of dates can correct a date someone remembers while preserving their standing to recount the experience and what it meant to them. Recognizing the speaker’s standing does not make every factual proposition automatically true.

Research topics. 02.01 Empiricism and rationalism (Hume, Descartes, and others). 02.02 Falsification, paradigms, and research programs (Popper, Kuhn, and Lakatos). 02.03 Pragmatism (Peirce, James, and Dewey). 02.04 Realism, constructivism, and critical realism. 02.05 Phenomenology (Husserl, Heidegger, and Merleau-Ponty). 02.06 Hermeneutics (Dilthey, Gadamer, and Ricœur). 02.07 Language, concepts, and context (Wittgenstein, Austin, and others). 02.08 Epistemic injustice and situated knowledge.

## D.03 Research Design, Measurement, Causation, and How Records Are Produced

In the main text, see §2.4–2.5, §4.1–4.6, §5.5.1, and §12.3–12.7. Research entries cover 03.01–03.09.

Randomized experiments, natural experiments, observational studies, process tracing, comparative history, and oral and archaeological materials each address some problems of identification. First specify the treatment, comparison object, time, outcome, and unit. The average effect for people invited, the change among actual participants, the effect of the policy as a whole, and one person’s outcome cannot stand in for one another merely because they share a program name.

Identification of a total effect does not require every mediator to have been identified. An account of a mechanism does not guarantee that there is a total effect. Controlling for the current state sometimes removes common causes, but it can also block a channel that formed over time or introduce selection bias. When H acts through S to affect Y and, given S, H adds no information, report the total effect and conditional residual separately. Do not take this to mean that history had no effect or that G3 is automatically satisfied.

Measurement must distinguish the object, observational indicators, constructs, and classification rules. Indicators that reflect a latent state and components that jointly define an index cannot use the same argument for validity. Changes in scale, denominator, measurement method, or rules for entering the sample can all change the trend. Better indicators can result from genuine improvement, narrow training, sample selection, or manipulation of records. Check these causes separately. The direction of the change does not settle its cause.

In a teaching example, the approval rate rises after an application process is simplified. That may indicate fewer procedural barriers. To judge whether people’s living conditions are better secured, also examine benefits actually delivered, service quality, and people who did not apply. Absence of objections in an archive has force as counterevidence only if relevant objections would have been recorded reliably and the records were preserved and accessible. The dates, uses, ownership, and actual power relations associated with material remains likewise cannot all be determined from one find.

Observation records are produced jointly by the object and the processes of collection and reporting. Fewer publicly recorded positive cases may result from lower actual risk, a response preventing outcomes, or a lower detection rate. If the available material is compatible with these processes, retain several explanations. Positive average effects on two segments in the same population do not suffice to establish a positive effect for the whole chain. The two segments may work for different people. Identify total effects, particular mediators, joint responses, and reporting mechanisms separately for the question at hand.

Research topics. 03.01 Correlation, causation, and identification of mechanisms. 03.02 Experiments, natural experiments, and observational studies. 03.03 Case studies, process tracing, and comparative history. 03.04 Statistical modeling, Bayesian updating, and uncertainty. 03.05 Measurement validity and proxy indicators. 03.06 Goodhart and Campbell problems. 03.07 Replication, publication bias, and controversies over classic experiments. 03.08 Historical materials, archives, oral history, and silence. 03.09 Archaeology, material culture, and societies without writing.

## D.04 Systems, Control, Feedback, Resilience, and Time Lags

In the main text, see §3.3, §4.5–4.6, §5.5, §6, and §12.6. Research entries cover 04.01–04.12.

A system representation is valuable when it links stocks, flows, feedback, delays, constraints, and boundaries to a specific object. Feedback requires only that a return affect a subsequent state. It does not guarantee accurate information, a timely response, or a beneficial outcome. An organization learning a practice, an emergent pattern in a system, insufficiency of the current representation, and cross-scale causation are four judgments that require separate evidence.

Observability and controllability must be assessed relative to the task. In a linear example, let O be the observation operator and Lx the target quantity. To identify the target, it suffices that unobservable directions leave the target unchanged, meaning every direction in ker(O) lies in ker(L). Reconstructing the full state need not be necessary. The rank of a control matrix can describe reachability in an ideal model. It does not guarantee execution under actual time limits, input bounds, resources, and permissions.

The same network diagram can give different propagation patterns under different response rules. Delays can stop a process with deadlines, forgetting, or resource depletion. In the special case of unlimited waiting, monotonic accumulation, and no forgetting, delay need not change the final reach. Resilience describes the maintenance of a particular relation or function. It does not automatically establish that the institution maintained is normatively justified, or make a return to the previous state the only goal for people’s lives.

A teaching model of stocks helps distinguish a temporary balance of flows from sustainable capacity. A shrinking backlog still calls for checking whether it reflects lower demand, greater processing capability, a change in the statistical boundary, or outsourcing of tasks. A short-term overshoot does not identify all time lags from the curve alone. General adaptation and learning can still occur when the current state captures the relevant history. Feedback-mediated learning through a carrier and learning with a historical increment are judged separately.

The capability to act also requires checking joint feasibility. Two tasks that each require one unit of capacity and have deadlines in the same period can each be feasible on their own, yet cannot both be completed with total capacity of one in that period. Adding capacity or legitimately staggering the deadlines changes feasibility. When one observation corresponds to several states, the existence of a correct action for each state does not guarantee a common available action when those states cannot be distinguished. Investigation, scheduling, and feedback should serve strategies that can actually be carried out.

Research topics. 04.01 General systems theory and systems thinking (Bertalanffy and Meadows). 04.02 Cybernetics (Wiener and Ashby). 04.03 Second-order cybernetics and observer reflexivity. 04.04 Complex adaptive systems and emergence. 04.05 Network coupling, cascades, and nonlinearity. 04.06 Resilience and adaptive cycles (Holling and others). 04.07 Path dependence, lock-in, and lags (Arthur and others). 04.08 Organizational ecology and system life cycles. 04.09 Observability and measurement blind spots. 04.10 Controllability and actual capability to intervene. 04.11 The good regulator theorem and internal models. 04.12 System dynamics, stocks, time lags, and overshoot.

## D.05 Information, Representation, and Borrowing From Mathematics and Physics

In the main text, see §2.6 and 2.8, §4.6 and 4.8, §5, and Appendix C. Research entries cover 05.01–05.08.

A representation must be evaluated relative to the question. Whether source states can be recovered, the specified task can be completed, computing costs are bearable, future dynamics have closure, and the representation remains sufficient after permitted interventions are five distinct dimensions. Losing source information need not cause the current task to fail. Sufficiency for one task cannot be extrapolated to all future questions.

Formally, a function g satisfying q=g∘f exists if and only if q takes the same value on every preimage of an individual value under f. This is a criterion for representational sufficiency. Actual computability is judged separately. The criterion gives a positive condition for abstraction to work in addition to warning about losses. If only one hidden share affects the outcome after intervention, that share can be added. Failure of closure need not lead to an endless attempt to recover every detail. Actual adoption should also account for the costs of measurement, privacy, maintenance, and understanding.

Unit conversions, accounting identities, empirical conversion rates, and value weights are established separately. Conservation of energy or matter requires appropriate boundaries and dimensions. Social burdens, suffering, attention, and moral responsibility do not acquire a conservation law by sharing the word “load.” Eliminating duplicated work, improving technology, or reducing harm can genuinely lower costs. It need not mean that costs were shifted elsewhere.

Results concerning Gödel, incompleteness, and computability depend on particular formal systems and kinds of problem. They do not imply that every finite task is undecidable. Model parsimony also depends on more than parameter count. Comparisons should include the full selection process, representations searched, tuning budget, and changes in objectives. Results in physics or mathematics can supply precise rules for bridging. Their prestige cannot substitute for empirical premises in the target domain.

Successive conversions must keep the same final question. If the first step is sufficient, the second must still preserve the distinctions needed for the final answer among the intermediate representations actually reachable. Completing two different local tasks separately does not establish that their composition still answers the original question. Check separately whether the answer function exists, whether an effective algorithm exists, whether it can be computed within budget, whether material is available in time, and whether actual use is correct. A complete finite table can be looked up. On a general infinite domain, preservation of information does not imply an algorithm that always halts.

Research topics. 05.01 Shannon information theory, channels, and noise. 05.02 Compression, representation, and lossy recasting. 05.03 Entropy, dissipative structures, and analogies to social order. 05.04 Borrowing conservation, potential fields, force, and carrying. 05.05 Gödel, incompleteness, and computability. 05.06 Effective theories, scale closure, and coarse-graining. 05.07 The historical retirement of information ontology, quantum analogies, and cosmological analogies. 05.08 Model selection, parsimony, and expressive power.

## D.06 Human Nature, Evolution, Habits, and the Capability to Act

In the main text, see §7, §8.1, §9, and §14.2–14.3. Research entries cover 06.01–06.08.

Present function, historical adaptation, drift, cultural transmission, individual intent, and consequences for welfare answer different questions. A behavior that is beneficial now was not necessarily produced initially for that function. Retention of a cultural form does not automatically show that every member endorses it. Kinship, reciprocity, reputation, partner choice, and institutional constraints can each produce cooperation. Specify who observes, who responds, and who bears the cost.

Personality differences can appear as conditionally stable tendencies. Slow change does not establish that a tendency is inherited, fixed, or the person’s essence. Repeated practice, embodied skills, opportunities in the environment, and contextual cues jointly shape behavior. Looking at the particular situation first can be an order of investigation. Comparisons of personality, situation, and their interaction must use comparable information and evaluation conditions. Evidence across situations and time still has to be distinguished from selection into the settings and the methods of measurement.

Action under constraints can still be a decision made by an actor. This does not make it freely given consent. A judgment should separate actual behavior, information available at the time, local control, available options for refusal, consequences, and attribution of responsibility. In a teaching example, an employee signs a record under a credible threat of punishment. The employee did in fact sign it. The people who designed the organization, the supervisor, and those who benefit may have distinct responsibilities. Saying “it was their own choice” cannot erase the chain of control.

High costs do not automatically establish sincerity, love, or identification. Goodwill at low cost also does not lose its value because it falls outside HV11. Practice can incorporate a skill into the current state. Task performance and evidence of how the skill formed can support a claim of learning in general. Whether there is also a historical increment beyond the current state is a separate question for the narrower G3 contract. Neither natural selection nor filtering by social systems directly establishes what ought to be done.

The explanatory contributions of situation, personality, and their interaction may overlap. A comparison must not assign the overlap entirely to one and then take a zero remainder for the other as proof that it does not exist. Behavioral differences produced by a treatment must be explained with reference to the entire set of conditions manipulated. Do not relabel them as the independent effect of one component without grounds. Whether someone under constraints actually acted, could safely refuse, validly consented, and bears a particular responsibility are still four distinct judgments.

Research topics. 06.01 Theories of human nature, including *xing shan* 性善 (human nature as good) and *xing e* 性恶 (human nature as bad), and situationism. 06.02 Natural selection, adaptation, and drift. 06.03 Cultural evolution and dual inheritance (Robert Boyd, Peter Richerson, and others). 06.04 Evolution of cooperation, kinship, and reciprocity. 06.05 Personality differences and interaction with situations. 06.06 Intent, freedom, responsibility, and the capability to act. 06.07 Habits, embodied practice, and shaping by the environment. 06.08 Action beyond incentives and resistance to reductionism.

## D.07 Bounded Rationality, Emotion, Group Decisions, and Exploration

In the main text, see §9, §11.5–11.8, and §12. Research entries cover 07.01–07.08.

Bounded rationality first asks what information, time, attention, and resources for execution are available when people decide. Determining what is normatively optimal, describing actual choice, and recommending improvements are three distinct tasks. Framing effects, reference points, beliefs, probability weighting, and utility curvature cannot be replaced by the label “irrational.” Given the same likelihood information, different priors can reasonably produce different posteriors.

Professional intuition can be useful in environments with learnable regularities and effective feedback. Seniority and confidence alone do not establish accuracy. Emotions can carry clues about risk and express values, and they can affect the actions available. Calm is no proof of being right, and an intense reaction is no proof of being wrong. Memory, present preferences, real future constraints, and fatigue from execution need to be distinguished.

Group judgment must distinguish independence of members’ responses, independence of relationships, independence of factual sources, and independence of errors. A majority of majorities can differ from the majority of all individuals. Several opinions can also repeat the same source. In a teaching example, each subgroup contributes one representative vote. This compresses away differences in group size and variation within the groups. To choose an aggregation rule, first state the evaluation objective and what qualifies someone to represent a group. “Collective wisdom” cannot substitute for a rule.

A small exploratory step has positive value only if information can change an executable plan in time, risk is bearable, and stopping or substitution is genuinely feasible. The value of knowledge need not all be converted into gains from action, but an investigation undertaken for a decision must weigh the costs of delay and privacy. Past investments that cannot be recovered differ from relocation, care, and contract costs and retaliation risks still to be faced. They must not all be treated as sunk-cost bias.

The evidential value of a message depends on the structure of its sources and the process by which it was included. The same nine positive reports can be repetitions of one underlying source or come from several conditionally independent observations. Their value for social coordination may still change as the number of supporters grows. Information that is free to obtain and can be ignored, and that leaves the objective and action set unchanged, has nonnegative gross value under an optimal policy. Investigation costs, lost opportunities, loss of privacy, and incorrect use enter the actual net value.

Research topics. 07.01 Bounded rationality and satisficing (Simon). 07.02 Heuristics, prospect theory, and framing effects (Kahneman and Tversky). 07.03 Naturalistic decision-making and professional intuition. 07.04 Belief updating, motivated reasoning, and cognitive dissonance. 07.05 Emotion, risk perception, and bodily states. 07.06 Attention, memory, and time preferences. 07.07 Group decision-making, groupthink, and collective wisdom. 07.08 Reversible choices, exploration, and the value of information.

## D.08 Development, Psychological Experience, Theories of Therapy, and Professional Boundaries

In the main text, see §2.11, §9, §13.5, and §16.12–16.14. Research entries cover 08.01–08.16.

Research on development, attachment, psychodynamics, learning, trauma, family systems, support, and diagnosis, along with specific approaches such as IFS, CBT, DBT, EMDR, mentalization, and schema therapy, cannot be combined into one ladder of personality maturity. Each study must first retain its own object, which might be subjective experience, a rating scale, a clinical classification, a process of formation, a mechanism of maintenance, a therapy package as a whole, or a particular component.

A beneficial therapy package does not establish that every mechanism proposed by its theory has been identified. Disagreement about mechanisms does not automatically cancel already supported limited effects. The therapeutic relationship, resources for daily living, pain, practice, and social support should retain their actual roles. Bodily relaxation, less intense emotion, or a more coherent account cannot individually authenticate a historical memory.

The framework provides only nonclinical limits for recording states, circumstances, privacy, and responsibility. It does not diagnose illness from fast-changing emotions, slow variables, or conflicts in relationships. It also does not treat refusal to disclose as evidence of pathology. Professional diagnosis, eligibility for administrative benefits, self-identification, and life goals must be judged separately. The absence of an ideal “healthy past” does not prevent discussion of change grounded in current safety, needs, and conditions of support.

In a teaching example, someone can plan daily life more easily after obtaining stable housing, although a symptom score has yet to change. The housing outcome can stand while the clinical conclusion remains limited. An unchanged score must not be recorded as an improvement or taken to negate the value of housing. Clinical judgment and high-risk care still require professional procedures. Professional qualifications do not certify a person’s “essence.”

Needing support does not disqualify an outcome. If the task is to express one’s wishes, continuing assistance can be part of how the task is completed. If the task is independent transfer of a skill, it requires a separate, appropriate assessment without assistance. The effects of a therapy package, comparisons of components, statistical mediation, a mechanism specific to the therapy, and applicability to an individual each admit their own limited answers. One answer cannot settle the others. Lack of proof of a distinctive mechanism cannot revoke a limited effect already established.

Research topics. 08.01 Developmental psychology (Piaget, Vygotsky, and Erikson). 08.02 Attachment, relational safety, and separation (Bowlby and Ainsworth). 08.03 Psychodynamics and the unconscious (Freud, Jung, and their critics). 08.04 Learning, reinforcement, and learned helplessness. 08.05 Trauma, stress, dissociation, and recovery. 08.06 Family systems and intergenerational transmission. 08.07 Recovery, therapy, and social support. 08.08 Diagnostic classification, neurodiversity, and stigma. 08.09 Polyvagal and body-oriented theories. 08.10 Specific models of psychodynamic personality structure. 08.11 Internal Family Systems (IFS). 08.12 Cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT). 08.13 Dialectical behavior therapy (DBT). 08.14 EMDR and integration of experience. 08.15 Mentalization and mentalization-based therapy. 08.16 Schema therapy.

## D.09 Love, Care, Recognition, Meaning, and Quality of Life

In the main text, see §1.8, §7.3.11, §9.7, §14.3, and Appendix A.11. Research entries cover 09.01–09.09.

Love can be an experience, a relationship, an action, or a normative understanding. Care can involve identifying needs, taking up the work, carrying it out, and responding. Recognition concerns standing, respect, and life together. Their shared concern with people does not make them a single functional variable. Private companionship, quietly watching, rest, hope, or mourning need not produce a detectable change in group structure to be meaningful.

Voluntariness, fairness, capability, and outcomes require separate evidence. Someone’s willingness to help does not establish a fair distribution of burdens. Low-cost help can still express goodwill, and considerable effort does not establish that refusal was freely available. HV11 is used to describe a particular bounded undertaking and its conditional consequences. It supplies neither a definition of all love nor a scale for judging personality from cost.

Death, dispersal, the end of a relationship, and organizational exit concern different objects and clocks. The end of a legal relationship, restoration of living arrangements, and changes in mourning can occur at different times. Organizational X0 cannot be applied as a model of a person’s death or universal stages of grief. Hope can support action, but wishes about the future, probability judgments, plans, and revisable commitments still have to be kept separate.

In a teaching example, direct contact may have to stop when someone leaves a relationship, while children or other dependents still need care to continue safely. Continuity of care cannot justify forcing someone who has been harmed to remain exposed. A justified exit also does not remove the need to check bounded duties that have independent grounds. Well-being, dignity, freedom from harm, and meaning can be evaluated alongside one another. People must not be compelled to accept a total score that decides every aspect of their lives for them.

Taking up the work of helping someone may involve doing a task, noticing needs, arranging, reminding, checking, and being on call. Completing one task can be genuine help. A claim that it reduces the burden over time also requires checking who does the coordination work. The fact that someone else can do the work does not make the person loved replaceable. Fewer tasks need not take away the sense of loss. An undertaking remains eligible to be described and evaluated even if it does not have the intended consequences.

Research topics. 09.01 Love as experience, relationship, and action. 09.02 Care ethics (Gilligan, Tronto, and others). 09.03 Recognition, dignity, and intersubjectivity (Hegel, Honneth, and others). 09.04 Existentialism, the absurd, and meaning (Kierkegaard, Sartre, Camus, Frankl, and others). 09.05 Death, mourning, memory, and endings. 09.06 Sacrifice, gifts, devotion, and depletion of the self. 09.07 Hope, faith, commitment, and the future. 09.08 Loneliness, belonging, friendship, and intimacy. 09.09 Suffering, happiness, desire, and quality of life.

## D.10 Ethical Disagreement, Capabilities, and Bounded Responsibility

In the main text, see §14, §15.2, and §16.2. Research entries cover 10.01–10.08.

Virtue ethics emphasizes practical judgment and how an actor lives. Deontology attends to constraints against treating people merely as means. Consequentialism compares the benefits and harms an action causes. Each tradition also has its own internal disputes. The framework should bring the reasons to bear on the same specific problem. It should not declare that one theory prevails in every task. The same facts can leave genuine normative disagreements. There is no need to invent empirical effects to eliminate them.

Resources, achievable capabilities, choices, and actual living outcomes each have their own conditions of conversion. With the same amount of money, people in different circumstances of care, disability, transport, and institutions may have different opportunities. Several capabilities that can each be maximized separately may not all be realizable together. Comparisons should retain genuinely feasible combinations. Being willing to accept, being able to afford it, and having no other option are also distinct conditions.

Adding utilities across people requires disclosing scales and weights. The possibility of compensation does not amount to compensation actually delivered. Freedom can be understood in terms of the extent of current intervention or evaluated by whether someone is subject to arbitrary power. The latter is a normative choice that must be explained. It is not a physical fact. Cultural differences do not automatically cancel shared protections. Claims for protection must also address different ways of living and the costs of implementation.

Responsibility should distinguish attribution of conduct, fault, causal contribution, benefit, preventive capability, and remedy. An institution’s ability to help does not make its duties unlimited. Lack of fault also need not rule out a remedy with independent grounds. A tragic choice may have no option without loss. Fully recording the requirements left unmet and the responsibilities that follow makes accountability possible in a way that deleting the costs from the metrics does not.

In a teaching example, a care facility has only one care worker on duty at night, two residents need help at the same time, delay carries an irreversible risk for both, and there is no option without loss. The record should state the decision rule relied on, for example attending first to the resident whose danger is more imminent. The other resident’s claim still holds, and the facility must afterward explain and provide remedy. The record must also state who decided on the constraint of staffing only one person at night and whether it should be reconsidered. The loss to the resident attended later cannot be recorded as zero, nor can it be used to conclude that the care worker failed in their duty.

Under the same facts, an aggregate principle and independent constraints concerning basic standing can permit different ranges of action. The analysis should disclose this normative disagreement. It must not use unsupported claims about future collapse to establish that every arrangement lacking justification is necessarily inefficient. A weighted sum can represent outcomes mathematically without determining units across people, social weights, or which losses may be offset. Bounded procedural duties can be fulfilled while irreversible losses in people’s lives remain.

Research topics. 10.01 Virtue ethics, deontology, and consequentialism. 10.02 Utilitarianism and commensurability of values. 10.03 Justice, fairness, and institutional choice (Rawls and critics). 10.04 The capability approach (Sen and Nussbaum). 10.05 Freedom, non-domination, and community. 10.06 Human rights, cultural differences, and universality. 10.07 Responsibility, remedy, and intergenerational and ecological justice. 10.08 Moral dilemmas, tragic choices, and incomparability.

## D.11 Modern Reinterpretations of Chinese Thought and Traditions of Practice

In the main text, see §2.6–2.7, §9, §12, and §14. Research entries cover 11.01–11.10.

Confucian accounts of roles, self-cultivation, and criticism raise different questions from Daoist accounts of *ziran* 自然 (naturalness), *qiwu* 齐物 (equalizing things), and the limits of knowledge. Mohist *jian ai* 兼爱 (impartial care), *fei gong* 非攻 (opposition to aggression), and practical efficacy raise further questions, as do Legalist rules, organization, and positional power, and military strategists’ attention to circumstances, information, and timing. Borrowing from these traditions should preserve the historical conditions and tensions within their texts. Their terms must not be reduced to synonyms for concepts the modern framework already has.

Role responsibilities can sustain care and can also be used to conscript it. Predictable rules can constrain local arbitrariness while central power remains unchecked. Less control can improve lives without implying that necessary support should be withdrawn. Effective military strategies provide no permission to move coercive methods into ordinary relationships. Different interpretations should be tested against specific objects and outcomes.

Sincerity does not guarantee an accurate factual judgment, and knowing a principle does not guarantee an opportunity to implement it. Other people’s willingness to listen is a separate matter from whether institutions let dissent safely change a decision. In a teaching example, an increase in local supplies on the books can support a claim about changes in inventory. Judging residents’ circumstances still requires tracing who is eligible, what they actually receive, its quality, and control within households. Calling the policy *minben* 民本 (taking the people as the foundation) cannot substitute for those checks.

*Lixue* 理学 (the learning of principle) and *xinxue* 心学 (the learning of the mind), *jingshi* 经世 (statecraft) and institutional practice, interpretations of *shiwei* 时位 (timing and position) in the *Zhouyi* 周易, and transformations in modern Chinese thought require separate treatment of theoretical principles, historical facts, methodological insights, and normative reasons. Insights from *tongbian* 通变 (understanding and adapting to change) do not automatically entail accepting the premises of divination. Modern interpretations in terms of equality or subjecthood also do not establish that earlier authors held exactly the same concepts. Traditions can be criticized, reinterpreted, and developed further. The framework cannot end the debate by calling them “compatible.”

An institution in a tradition can use different arrangements for eligibility, examinations, appointments, and actual opportunities to enter. Equality in one dimension does not establish equality overall. A difference in one dimension does not establish differences in the same direction at every step. Written rules, local implementation, and the experience of people affected support distinct conclusions. Modern reinterpretation can favor equality and independent review, but must acknowledge these as present normative choices. They cannot be written back as the entirety of the earlier authors’ meaning.

Research topics. 11.01 Confucian *ren* 仁 (humaneness), *li* 礼 (ritual propriety), roles, and relationships (Confucius, Mencius, and Xunzi). 11.02 Daoism (Laozi and Zhuangzi). 11.03 Mohism, *jian ai* 兼爱, *fei gong* 非攻, and *shang xian* 尚贤 (elevating the worthy). 11.04 Legalism, *fa* 法 (standards), *shu* 术 (techniques of rule), and *shi* 势 (positional power). 11.05 Military thought and strategy (Sun Tzu and others). 11.06 Song–Ming *lixue* 理学 and *xinxue* 心学. 11.07 *Jingshi* 经世, *minben* 民本, institutions, and local practice. 11.08 The *Zhouyi* 周易, *shiwei* 时位, and choices about what to retain from *shushu* 术数 (divinatory and numerological practices). 11.09 Transformations in modern and contemporary Chinese thought. 11.10 Modern reinterpretations of Chinese thought.

## D.12 Religion, Comparisons Across Civilizations, and Relational Knowledge

In the main text, see §2.7, §5.10, §7, §10, and §16.15. Research entries cover 12.01–12.13.

Greek philosophy, Judaism and Christianity, Islamic thought, South Asian traditions, Buddhism across cultures, secularization, African thought, Indigenous knowledge, Latin American liberation thought, and Southeast Asian and Pacific experience must not be treated as civilizations with a single will. Preserve textual doctrines, institutional rules, personal beliefs, state power, reform movements, and practices of everyday life separately.

Relational knowledge can include detailed environmental observation, skills, and arrangements for responsibility. Religious and philosophical arguments can also contribute understanding and normative value of their own. A skill can genuinely work without researchers first identifying every mechanism. Its success does not establish every cosmological explanation associated with it. Moral, political, and empirical claims are subject to criticism on their own terms.

Being able to locate knowledge, being able to make it public, having permission to reuse it, and having authority to represent its source community are distinct qualifications. Non-disclosure for protection does not mean the knowledge is absent. Understanding a term does not give an outside researcher permission to publish sensitive material or make decisions for the community. A translation may suffice for one task while leaving some parts unaligned for another.

In a teaching example, a community’s navigational practices reliably help with a kind of task, and an outside model also explains some of the trajectories. Both sides can exchange knowledge and examine their own errors. Explicit equations do not give the outside model authority over decisions in daily life. A cultural identity does not exempt the local method from concrete checks on safety and effectiveness. Local institutional reform can coexist with continuing exclusion. Comparison across civilizations must not be turned into an overall ranking.

Comparability across periods depends on the particular item and use. Changing the wording or mode of one survey item can limit its direct comparison while leaving other trends that have a basis for bridging. Following up on the same random assignment adds information over time, without increasing the number of independent trials. A statistically significant result in one period and a nonsignificant one in another cannot substitute for a test of the difference between periods. Limited improvements in skills or services can be real, while control over one’s own agenda, the ability to refuse, and access to remedy still require separate evaluation.

Research topics. 12.01 Greek philosophy (Plato, Aristotle, and the Stoic tradition). 12.02 Jewish and Christian traditions. 12.03 Islamic thought and political community. 12.04 Indian thought (Buddhism, Hinduism, Jainism, and others). 12.05 The spread of Buddhism across cultures. 12.06 Secularization, religious reform, and religious revival. 12.07 African thought and traditions such as Ubuntu. 12.08 Indigenous and relational knowledge. 12.09 Latin American liberation thought and decolonial criticism. 12.10 Comparisons across civilizations and points of nonalignment in translation. 12.11 Caste, social hierarchy, and egalitarian reform in South Asia. 12.12 The autonomous histories and intellectual practices of Southeast Asian societies. 12.13 Pacific island societies, navigation, and relational knowledge.

## D.13 Kinship, Exchange, Ritual, Ethnography, and Cultural Change

In the main text, see §2.5–2.7, §7.5, §9.7, §10, and §16.6. Research entries cover 13.01–13.08.

Kinship rules, gifts and debts, rituals and classifications, habitus and capital, ecology and livelihoods, and ethnography each retain their own units of analysis. Nonmarket exchange does not entail an absence of obligations. Gifts and commodities can also be intertwined within one relationship. Ritual coordination, public identity, private belief, and long-term cooperation can be out of sync. Attending together cannot establish all those outcomes.

Symbolic interpretation should explain how a practice is understood in context. Material and institutional research traces resources, labor, constraints, and consequences. These approaches can check each other without substituting for each other. Habitus can develop stable tendencies through long practice and can also change through multiple experiences of socialization and changes in the environment. Conversion of capital requires recognition and rules within a particular field. It does not operate as an unconditional transfer of energy.

Access for ethnographic work, trust, recording, publication, and authority to represent people each require their own grounds. The detail of thick description can show researchers experiences a questionnaire misses. Familiarity with a field does not give them knowledge of every member’s wishes. How the observer’s presence changes expression, who was not contacted, and who cannot speak safely should all remain part of the explanation. Calling the material “subjective” cannot justify dismissing it all.

In a teaching example, a festival introduces a new medium, changing participation and income. Some skills are preserved, while other experiences no longer occur. Genuine continuing cultural creation can coexist with definite losses. “New creation” does not amount to faking a tradition. “Preserving tradition” does not establish the absence of internal control or problems of distribution. Disagreements between cultural relativism and shared protections should be addressed in arguments about the particular case.

Strong commitments by original authors must be preserved as stated in their texts. When borrowing a local analysis of symbolic interpretation, material conditions, or conversion of capital, do not first reduce all its claims about priority, a common scale, or overall coordination to neutral reminders and then declare the traditions compatible. The presence of ritual, physiological synchrony, expressions of identity, accuracy of memory, and sustained cooperation each have their own units of evidence. Shared rituals and repeated use of the same underlying sample cannot count as several independent validations merely because the results appear in separate publications.

Research topics. 13.01 Kinship, exchange, and gifts (Mauss, Lévi-Strauss, and others). 13.02 Ritual, liminality, and a sense of commonality (Durkheim, van Gennep, and Victor Turner). 13.03 Symbols, classification, and interpretation of culture (Geertz and Douglas). 13.04 Habitus, field, and capital (Bourdieu). 13.05 Cultural materialism, ecology, and livelihoods. 13.06 Ethnography, thick description, and participant observation. 13.07 Cultural relativism and shared protections. 13.08 Cultural change, mixing, and revival.

## D.14 Social Theory, Power, Communication, and Institutional Reproduction

In the main text, see §3.3, §7.6, §10–11, §14.5, and §16.2. Research entries cover 14.01–14.09.

Formation, maintenance, function, social recognition, legal validity, and normative justification are distinct questions. Functionalist accounts of consequences, the meanings of action in interpretive sociology, the order of encounters in interactionism, temporal processes of structuration and morphogenesis, studies of power and knowledge, public communication, social systems theory, and actor–network theory cannot all be reduced to one measure of “structural strength.”

Visible compliance can arise from endorsement, habit, dependence on income, threats, or lack of alternatives. The same behavior does not suffice to identify a motive. Understanding a message does not amount to agreement. Establishing a legal rationale in writing does not mean services have reached people. The causal effects of objects and technologies do not make them bear human moral responsibility. Rules can constrain action and can also make coordination and claims to rights possible.

Criticism must trace concrete channels of classification, training, recordkeeping, rewards and penalties, and decision-making authority. Participation can correct mistakes or increase expression without changing permissions. A reasoned refusal of a request through an ordinary procedure can still have value for respect and knowledge. For adoption within the framework, GOV-02 requires each item to be addressed with reasons, independent review where needed, and verification of actual changes. Token acceptance cannot establish `applied`. The continued presence of objections must not invariably be treated as a permanent veto.

In a teaching example, an appeal is heard and receives a reasoned response. The original decision stands because it has sufficient evidence. This can establish that a response actually took place, but does not establish that the applicant’s request was granted. Another appeal actually changes rules and resources. The change alone does not establish that the distribution is normatively justified. Record causal feedback, ordinary participation, and authorization to adopt a new version within the framework at three separate levels.

Institutions can be realized repeatedly through skilled practice, budgets, tools, informal commitments, and personnel arrangements. They need not rewrite their charters each time. Reorganization of actual processes while documents stay the same, and changes to documents while execution stays the same, both have mechanisms that can be studied. Upholding a correct decision after review and leaving a decision in place without genuine review can look the same. They remain distinguishable by reasons, handling of material, and authority. The rate of changes cannot substitute for judging quality.

Research topics. 14.01 Social facts and functionalism (Durkheim, Parsons, and Merton). 14.02 Interpretive sociology (Weber). 14.03 Symbolic interactionism and everyday order (Mead, Blumer, and Goffman). 14.04 Structuration, morphogenesis, and agency (Giddens and Archer). 14.05 Power, knowledge, discipline, and governmentality (Foucault). 14.06 Critical theory and public communication (the Frankfurt School and Habermas). 14.07 Social systems theory (Luhmann). 14.08 Conflict, integration, and institutional reproduction. 14.09 Actor–network theory and nonhuman positions of action (Latour).

## D.15 Games, Cooperation, Signals, Commitment, and Implementation

In the main text, see §10.5, §11.3 and 11.7, §12, and §14. Research entries cover 15.01–15.12.

A game representation starts by specifying actual decision nodes, information, feasible choices, outcomes, and evaluation. Being included among the players does not confer full authority to represent others. People outside the player set may still bear costs and have rights. A third party that can change permissions or resources should appear in the causal model. If it currently has no node at which it can exercise control, it still cannot be removed from the normative allocation.

Equilibrium existence, equilibrium selection, actual attainment, dynamic stability, efficiency, and normative justification each require their own grounds. Repeated interaction can sustain cooperation and can also undermine it through false alarms, short-term incentives, or uncontrolled punishment. A costly signal can distinguish types only if the recipient’s interpretation fits each type’s incentives to choose it and ability to bear its cost.

Distinguish the announcement of a commitment, reasons to honor it later, and the actual ability to honor it when the time comes. Deterrence differs from demanding reversal of an action already taken. Neither is shown to work by a forceful declaration alone. An assurance that the threat can end after compliance also matters. Truthful reporting in mechanism design does not automatically address hidden action, the incentives of overseers, multiple equilibria, or authority to implement.

In a teaching example, a rule gets members to report their needs truthfully but leaves oversight to people who benefit from violations. Reporting can work while implementation still fails. Revise the particular actions and chain of incentives. A general claim that rational models are useless does not identify what needs changing. The core, Shapley allocations, and bargaining solutions answer different questions about cooperation. They do not by themselves produce a unique fair allocation. An actual violation enters e(t). Simulating a violation does not confer permission to carry it out.

A guarantee about a mechanism must state the set of permitted actions. Actions permitted by the rules may differ from actions that can actually be taken. An unprofitable deviation in the former set does not show that violations in the latter are no longer attractive. A guarantee of truthful reporting also does not automatically cover hidden implementation, shared funding, or losses to third parties. After adding implementers and resource constraints, solve the corresponding mechanism again and retain the original guarantee within the scope of its original conditions.

Research topics. 15.01 Limits of game representations. 15.02 Nash equilibrium and coordination dilemmas. 15.03 Repeated games, reputation, and cooperation. 15.04 Signaling, screening, and cheap talk (Spence and others). 15.05 Incomplete information, beliefs, and chains of suspicion (Harsanyi and others). 15.06 Commitment, deterrence, and bargaining (Schelling and others). 15.07 Public choice, free riding, and collective action (Olson and Buchanan). 15.08 Mechanism design and implementation (Hurwicz, Maskin, and Myerson). 15.09 Cooperative games, coalitions, and allocation. 15.10 Evolutionary games and learning dynamics. 15.11 Network games and interaction across levels. 15.12 Conditions for stopping a game-theoretic explanation.

## D.16 Organizations, Work, Leadership, and Labor Protection

In the main text, see §6.3–6.5, §7.6, §8, and §15.3.3. Research entries cover 16.01–16.09.

Formation, growth, differentiation, exploration and exploitation, resource dependence, institutionalization, and exit in organizations need to be connected to tasks, knowledge, resources, permissions, and actual chains of work. Organizational life-cycle models can raise questions about conditions at different stages. They cannot put all organizations into a necessary sequence. Legal entities, work networks, knowledge, and services need not begin or end together.

For organizational learning, distinguish plan formation, record creation, actual use, durable change, and results. An unfinished test of one branch of feedback-mediated learning does not cancel a process of learning in general that can already be traced. A training session or adoption into institutional rules does not automatically show that later tasks improved. Isomorphic rules can support coordination and protection. Decoupling can occur in texts, implementation, or outcomes, and does not always amount to deception.

Professional and informal networks can make up for gaps in formal procedures or exclude people without connections. The role of leadership in an organization should be traced through agendas, resources, interpretation, authorization, and correction. Individual traits cannot account for every success or failure. Evaluation of labor must distinguish working hours, pace, skills, income, health, hidden maintenance, and care. Output cannot stand in for the quality of the work as a whole.

When comparing working-time data, check statutory limits, overtime pay thresholds, schedules, and hours actually worked separately for the populations, units of time, stages of implementation, and exceptions that apply.

Worker codetermination must apply to specific matters. Distinguish raising an issue, obtaining information, consultation, bargaining, joint agreement, adjudicating disputes, and making rules autonomously within an organization. Workers can develop their own mutual aid and working rules. Authority to change an employer’s or platform’s rules must be checked separately. Members, owners, employees, and those represented need not be the same people. One vote cannot substitute for nomination, accountability, and internal dissent.

In a teaching example, output recovers after a change in leadership, but a few people still fill the gaps by working overtime. Higher output is a real result. Sustainability, distribution, and continuity still need to be assessed. Exit, splitting, merger, and collective bargaining require checks on contracts, knowledge, the scope of representation, actual implementation, and anti-retaliation. Eligibility under the framework’s analysis must not be made a condition for workers to organize or appeal.

Failures of organizational memory can occur at different points in storage, retrieval, awareness, handling, and consequences. If old information has been retrieved but does not enter decision-making or action, calling the problem “forgetting” will miss an organizational blockage. The shock caused by the departure of a person in charge can identify a specific dependency. It cannot on its own describe the world in which that person was never there. Organizational persistence, the capability to do the work, procedural improvements, and workers’ living conditions are separate outcomes. Evidence for one does not establish the others.

Research topics. 16.01 Organizational life cycles (Greiner and Adizes). 16.02 Exploration and exploitation, and organizational learning (March, Argyris, and Schön). 16.03 Resource dependence and institutionalization (Pfeffer, Salancik, and Selznick). 16.04 Institutional isomorphism, decoupling, and organizational legitimacy (Meyer, Rowan, DiMaggio, and Powell). 16.05 Bureaucracy, professional organizations, and informal networks. 16.06 Leadership, entrepreneurship, and collective generation. 16.07 Labor processes, metrics, incentives, and burnout. 16.08 Exit, splitting, merger, and organizational memory. 16.09 Worker organizing, collective bargaining, and labor protection.

## D.17 Production, Exchange, Property Rights, Institutions, and Development

In the main text, see §2.8, §10.6.1, §12.10, §14.4, and §15.3.5. Research entries cover 17.01–17.08.

Classical political economy, Marxian political economy, neoclassical welfare analysis, the Austrian school, institutional economics, and development research raise different questions about production, value, exchange, knowledge, power, and historical change. The framework retains their domain-specific variables and disputes. General terms such as “flow” and “uptake” must not erase prices, property rights, labor processes, and institutional rules.

Market prices can organize dispersed information and exchange, but cannot by themselves account for welfare, genuine opportunities, or the distribution of rights. An accounting identity does not supply a causal mechanism. A title of ownership does not amount to actual control. A transaction taking place does not establish that both parties had comparable choices. Institutional comparisons should examine which concrete channels a rule changes, how alternatives operate, and what they cost. Changing abstract labels alone does not suffice.

Productive capacity, people’s substantive capabilities, and the state’s implementation capacity are distinct. Economic growth can support education, health, and choice, but converting resources into opportunities people can use still depends on eligibility, access, quality, and control by those concerned. Development paths also involve maintenance, care, the environment, and people who cannot move. Aggregate output cannot substitute for consequences at each position.

In a teaching example, a public service is counted in output at cost, yet residents still have difficulty using it. Its nonmarket status does not keep it out of the accounts, and inclusion in the accounts does not establish adequate quality. Unpaid labor has costs, and cooperative members do not necessarily include all workers. Analysis of redistribution should trace statutory eligibility, application, actual receipt, and outcomes in people’s lives. Preserve genuine improvements and remaining gaps at each step.

Comparisons of reforms must fix the baseline and transition path. A final state can leave no one worse off than the initial state while leaving some people worse off than at an intermediate stage of reform. A statically feasible arrangement does not settle investment, credible commitments, or later productive capacity. An economic comparator suited to the question should retain existing research on power, implementation, distribution, and cost. One must not first strip it down to prices or cash alone and then claim to complete it by adding fields.

Research topics. 17.01 Classical political economy (Smith and Ricardo). 17.02 Marxian political economy. 17.03 Neoclassical economics and welfare analysis. 17.04 The Austrian school (Mises, Hayek, and Rothbard). 17.05 Institutional economics (Coase, Williamson, and North). 17.06 Development economics and structural transformation. 17.07 The welfare state, redistribution, and social security. 17.08 Nonmarket economies, cooperatives, and mixed institutions.

## D.18 Money, Credit, Finance, and Crises

In the main text, see §2.8, §6.6 and 6.12–6.13, §10.6.2, and §12. Research entries cover 18.01–18.11.

Financial analysis must retain currency, maturity, priority, security, the parties involved, and the basis of valuation. A loan creates an asset and a corresponding liability. This does not amount to an equal increase in net wealth. Writing off an asset does not automatically erase another party’s deposit. Insufficient cash flow, impaired net worth, frozen financing channels, and legal payment requirements are distinct conditions. “Overload” cannot stand in for all of them.

Maturity transformation can support investment and liquidity. Runs can involve coordination risk and fundamental risk. Keynes’s account of demand and uncertainty, theories of money and expectations, Minsky’s financing structures, debt deflation and the financial accelerator, and behavioral and narrative research offer different conditional explanations. Together they do not establish an inevitable crisis cycle.

In a teaching example, collateral value falls from 100 to 80 on a loan of 60 with a maximum loan-to-value ratio of 70%. The permitted balance falls to 56, creating a need to make up 4. This does not establish a loss of 4. If a fire sale brings in only 45, the remaining repayment and loss are a separate accounting question. Collateral revaluation, top-up payments, sale, price feedback, and contraction of lending must each be explained. A fall in price alone cannot supply the entire chain.

Account separately for the amount authorized for a rescue, the amount actually paid out, risk bearing in advance, and the final net cost. A temporary liquidity bridge can be useful without establishing that the capital position or long-term business model has been repaired. An average household debt-service ratio cannot substitute for the high-burden tail. Essential expenses and buffers must also be examined. Record links among different versions, analyses of the same plan, and reanalyses of the same data. Do not count independent evidence by the number of papers.

The same quarter can have different values under different methods or releases. Joining one point under the old method to the next under the new method can create a change absent from a series using the same version throughout. Restore a common method or defensible bridge before explaining the mechanism. Contractual payments, actual payments, changes in balances, and cash pressure require separate evidence. Revising a version does not establish households’ actual burdens or a policy’s contribution.

Research topics. 18.01 Money, credit, and debt relations. 18.02 Banking, maturity transformation, and runs. 18.03 Keynes, effective demand, and uncertainty. 18.04 Monetarism, rational expectations, and policy responses. 18.05 Minsky, leverage, and financial instability. 18.06 Asset bubbles, narratives, and behavioral finance. 18.07 Sovereign debt, international currencies, and financial power. 18.08 Crisis rescues and distributional consequences. 18.09 The financial accelerator, collateral, and balance sheets. 18.10 Household debt and the debt-service ratio DSR. 18.11 Cash flow, maturity mismatch, and credit backstops.

## D.19 Political Thought, Institutional Visions, and Normative Disagreement

In the main text, see §2.2, §14, and §16.2 and 16.15. Research entries cover 19.01–19.08.

Liberalism, republicanism, democratic thought, conservatism, socialist traditions, anarchism, nationalism, and decolonial criticism raise different questions about freedom, equality, order, community, property, and authority. Comparisons should show their specific claims, institutional mechanisms, conditions of applicability, and internal disputes accurately. The framework should not impose an overall ranking on political choices.

Rules governing rights, powers that can be contested and revoked, the actual effect of participation, the costs of change and of the status quo, production, control of resources, local cooperation, and institutions spanning regions need separate descriptions. Nominal ownership, shared identity, and local autonomy do not directly establish actual control and distribution. A tradition’s attention to an issue does not mean that all other traditions ignore it.

Visions can help identify institutional possibilities that have yet to be realized. Dystopias can reveal risks of control. Neither automatically predicts the endpoint of history. A specific comparison requires the same object, period, and population. State who holds which rights, who decides, who bears the costs of transition, which outcomes have been observed, and which remain conditional inferences.

The framework’s `authorized` and `applied` statuses and its governance statuses are internal commitments. They cannot stand in for external legal validity or impose unanimous consent as the rule for every public decision. Conversely, external legal grounds do not automatically establish normative justification. Keeping this distinction lets comparisons of political thought inform people without making political decisions for them or using empirical claims to erase genuine conflicts of value.

Stability, efficiency, and whether an arrangement is worth maintaining can genuinely diverge. A normative critique need not become a prediction of inevitable failure. To assess representation and dispersal of power, trace nominations, agendas, information, voting, implementation, removal, and review. One gate with no substitute can create control without a monopoly at every step. Lawful institutional representation does not require fabricating private unanimous consent from each person. Its scope and accountability still require examination.

Research topics. 19.01 Lineages of liberalism (Locke, Mill, Berlin, Rawls, and others). 19.02 Republicanism and democratic thought. 19.03 Conservatism and tradition (Burke and others). 19.04 Socialism, Marxism, and social democracy. 19.05 Anarchism, mutual aid, and local autonomy. 19.06 Nationalism, identification with the state, and fascism. 19.07 Postcolonial and decolonial thought and dependency theory. 19.08 Utopias, dystopias, and narratives of the end of history.

## D.20 State Capacity, Policy Implementation, and Governance

In the main text, see §7.3.7–7.3.8, §10–11, §14, and §15.3.5. Research entries cover 20.01–20.10.

State formation, fiscal and military mobilization, administrative autonomy, embeddedness, legitimacy of rule, social identification, and public services are distinct processes. Compliance can have several causes. Administrative reach can coexist with gaps in rights. State capacity should be specified by task, level, and organizational resources. One aggregate measure of strength cannot substitute for this analysis.

A policy chain can distinguish entry of an issue onto the agenda, rulemaking, definition of eligibility, making budgets available, frontline implementation, notification and application, determining and paying benefits, actual use, and correction through appeal. The stages have different denominators and timing. Rates for eligible people, applicants, people approved, and actual recipients must not be mixed. Whether the calculation uses households or individuals can also change the result.

Frontline discretion can compensate for rules that do not fit or create bias and exclusion. Assess it in light of task conflicts, resources, and oversight. Polycentric governance requires actual authority and coordination. A large number of institutions does not suffice. Anti-corruption and oversight work must check who appoints, who pays, who selects the sample, and who has authority to implement and review. The value of local knowledge does not establish that local elites represent every resident.

In a teaching example, random inspections improve the completeness of records while remedies for ordinary applicants remain slow. Oversight effects and remedies in individual cases each have their own scope. Temporary aid can improve people’s lives while leaving questions about continuing finance and the right to choose. For an institutional externality, specify which decision accounting excludes it. Harm can be recorded before final legal responsibility is established.

Specify whom autonomy is relative to and what it concerns. Freedom from party control, discretion under administrative rules, and authority for local bodies or users to change rules are different dimensions. Ties to higher levels can provide resources and remedies or control goals and individual cases. Approval rates, the proportion who have ever received benefits, and continuously available services have different denominators and time conditions. Retain local improvements already observed. Successes among people inside the procedure cannot establish coverage of the entire eligible population.

Research topics. 20.01 State formation and fiscal-military states (Tilly and others). 20.02 Legitimacy, domination, and identification with the state (Weber and others). 20.03 State capacity, autonomy, and embeddedness (Mann, Evans, Fukuyama, and others). 20.04 Street-level bureaucracy and policy implementation (Lipsky). 20.05 Polycentric governance and the commons (Ostrom). 20.06 Public policy, implementation, and feedback. 20.07 Central–local relations and scale. 20.08 Corruption, capture, and principal–agent relations. 20.09 Comparisons of democratic, authoritarian, bureaucratic, and technocratic governance. 20.10 Governance failure, state fragility, and reconstruction.

## D.21 Law, Authorization, Procedure, and Responsibility

In the main text, see §2.4.3, §5.5.5 and 5.5.7, §11.3, §13.5, §14, and §15.2. Research entries cover 21.01–21.08.

Distinguish the validity of a source of law, its application to an individual case, moral reasons, actual authority, procedures for remedy, and what reaches people in practice. Natural law, positive law, legal realism, constitutionalism, and research on legal pluralism help analyze these questions in different ways. The existence of an institutional text does not establish implementation. A rule’s legal status does not automatically determine its normative evaluation.

Consent, authorization, agency, representation, and the right to bring a claim are distinct. Some public or statutory procedures do not depend on individual private mandates. This does not establish consent by those concerned. Personal consent also cannot substitute for the public authority an institution requires. A model’s recommendation, actual implementation, and later confirmation must be recorded separately. The fact that an observed act was unauthorized is no reason to erase it from the record.

Hearing people, respecting procedure, correcting evidence, changing a decision, paying compensation, and revising rules can occur at different times. A remedy in one case does not establish that the institution has been corrected throughout. Upholding a decision also does not necessarily mean that no one was heard. Punishment, prevention, compensation, truth, nonviolent coexistence, and forgiveness are distinct and cannot offset one another. T0 prohibits forced reconciliation. It does not rule out all lawful evidence gathering.

In a teaching example, an accurate annual income figure does not suffice to determine income in each short benefit window. Correct arithmetic cannot make up for a gap in timing information that matters under the law.
